Diagram automorphisms and canonical bases
for quantum affine algebras, II

Abstract
. Let 𝐔 q − \mathbf{U}_{q}^{-} be the negative part of the quantum enveloping algebra, and σ \sigma the algebra automorphism on 𝐔 q − \mathbf{U}_{q}^{-} induced from a diagram automorphism. Let 𝐔 ¯ q − \underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-} be the quantum algebra obtained from σ \sigma , and 𝐁 ~ \widetilde{\mathbf{B}} (resp. 𝐁 ¯ ~ \widetilde{\underline{\mathbf{B}}} ) the canonical signed basis of 𝐔 q − \mathbf{U}_{q}^{-} (resp. 𝐔 ¯ q − \underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-} ). Assume that 𝐔 q − \mathbf{U}_{q}^{-} is simply-laced of finite or affine type. In our previous papers [SZ1, 2], we have proved by an elementary method, that there exists a natural bijection 𝐁 ~ σ ≃ 𝐁 ¯ ~ \widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\simeq\widetilde{\underline{\mathbf{B}}} in the case where σ \sigma is admissible. In this paper, we show that such a bijection exists even if σ \sigma is not admissible, possibly except some small rank cases.



Introduction

This paper is a continuation of [SZ1, 2].
Let
X
be a simply-laced Cartan datum with the vertex set
I
, and
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}

the negative part of the quantum enveloping algebra associated to
X
.
We assume that
X
is irreducible, of finite or affine type,
and let
\sigma:I\to I
be the diagram automorphism of
X
. We further assume that

\sigma
is admissible (see 1.3 for the definition).
Then the order
\varepsilon
of
\sigma
is 2, 3 or 4.
\sigma
induces an algebra automorphism
of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
. Let
\underline{I}
be the set of
\sigma
-orbits in
I
.
One can
construct the Cartan datum
\underline{X}
with the vertex set
\underline{I}
. We denote by

\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
the negative part of the quantum enveloping algebra associated to

\underline{X}
. Let
\mathbf{B}
(resp.
\underline{\mathbf{B}}
) be the canonical basis of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}

(resp.
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
). Then
\sigma
acts on
\mathbf{B}
as a permutation. We denote by

\mathbf{B}^{\sigma}
the set of
\sigma
-fixed elements in
\mathbf{B}
. It is known by Lusztig [L1]
that there exists a natural bijection
\mathbf{B}^{\sigma}\simeq\underline{\mathbf{B}}
.

Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
, and
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
Lusztig’s integral form of

\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
, which is an
\mathbf{A}
-subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
. Assume that

\varepsilon=2,3
, and let
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{Z}/\varepsilon\mathbf{Z}
be the finite field of
\varepsilon
elements.
Set
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}=\mathbf{F}[q,q^{-1}]
, and define
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}
as the
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
-subalgebra
of
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbf{A}}{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}

consisting of
\sigma
-fixed elements.
The
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
-algebra
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
is defined as

\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbf{A}}{}_{\mathbf{A}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
.
In [SZ1, 2],
it is shown that, in the case where
\sigma
is admissible with
\varepsilon\neq 4
,
there exists an
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
-algebra isomorphism
\Phi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathbf{V}_{q}
,
where
\mathbf{V}_{q}
is a certain quotient algebra of
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}
.
By using this isomorphism, we have constructed, in an elementary way,
the canonical signed basis
\widetilde{\underline{\mathbf{B}}}=\underline{\mathbf{B}}\sqcup-\underline{\mathbf{B}}
of
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
,
and a bijection
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\simeq\widetilde{\underline{\mathbf{B}}}
, where
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}
is the canonical
signed basis of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.
This result was generalized in [MSZ] to the case where
X
is of general Kac-Moody type,

\sigma
is admissible, and
\varepsilon
is a power of a prime number.

In this paper, we consider the case where
\sigma
is not admissible.
Already in [L2], Lusztig proved that in the case where
X
is of finite type,
the bijection
\mathbf{B}^{\sigma}\simeq\underline{\mathbf{B}}
still holds. So, one can expect that
our isomorphism
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathbf{V}_{q}
will have a generalization
for non-admissible cases.
However, in the case where
X=A_{n}^{(1)}
, and
\sigma
is
the one corresponding to the cyclic quiver, there does not exist
\sigma
-stable
canonical basis (see Remark 1.9). So we must exclude this case.
Then we show in Theorem 2.6, that similar results as in [SZ1, 2] hold
for
X
of finite or affine type, possibly except

\underline{X}
is of type
A_{2}^{(2)}
or
A_{1}^{(1)}
.
The latter cases are excluded simply because
the computation becomes more complicated.
It is likely that our results will hold in those cases.

Note that our discussion in [SZ1, 2] heavily depends on the
property of PBW-bases.
But in the non-admissible case, the action of
\sigma
on PBW-bases becomes quite
different from the admissible case. The simplest example is that
X
is of type
A_{2}

and
\underline{X}
is of type
C_{1}
. In this case, there does not exist
\sigma
-stable PBW-bases
except the trivial one. Thus the usual PBW-bases are not so convenient for
the non-admissible case. The main ingredient for our discussion is
the modified PBW-bases, which is defined by mixing the usual PBW-bases and
the canonical bases for
A_{2}
. By using the modified PBW-bases, one can prove that
\Phi
is
a homomorphism. The injectivity of
\Phi
is proved by a general argument as in
[MSZ]. In the case where
X
is of finite type,
\sigma
acts on the set of modified
PBW-bases, and the surjectivity is proved in Theorem 3.7,
by a similar argument as in [SZ1].
In the affine case, the modified PBW-bases are constructed, by making use of
the PBW-bases associated to the convex order due to Muthiah-Tingley [MT] as in [SZ2].
Since it is not certain whether
\sigma
leaves
the set of modified PBW-bases invariant, we prove the surjectivity
in Proposition 6.21, by using some properties of Kashiwara operators.
Note, in contrast to the discussion in [SZ2], that we need to use modified PBW-bases
in order to define Kashiwara operators for the
\sigma
-setup.
Finally, we give an explicit description of
the bijection
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\widetilde{\underline{\mathbf{B}}}
in terms of modified PBW-bases.

1.
Diagram automorphisms

1.1.

We basically follow the notation in [MSZ, 1].
Let
X=(I,(\ ,\ ))
be a symmetric Cartan datum,
where
I
is a finite set, and
(\ ,\ )
is a symmetric bilinear form on
the vector space
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{Q}\alpha_{i}
with basis

\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I\}
. Hence the Cartan matrix

A=(a_{ij})
is symmetric, where

a_{ij}=\frac{2(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})}{(\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j})}
.
Let
Q=\sum_{i\in I}\mathbf{Z}\alpha_{i}
be the root lattice of
X
.
Set
Q_{+}=\sum_{i\in I}\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}\alpha_{i}
and
Q_{-}=-Q_{+}
.
Let
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
be the negative part of the quantized enveloping algebra

\mathbf{U}_{q}
over
\mathbf{Q}(q)
associated to
X
with generators
f_{i}\ (i\in I)
.
It is known by Lusztig [L1] that
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
has the
canonical basis
\mathbf{B}
.

Let
\sigma:I\to I
be a diagram automorphism on
X
, namely

\sigma
satisfies the property
(\alpha_{\sigma(i)},\alpha_{\sigma(j)})=(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})

for any
i,j\in I
.
Then
\sigma
induces an automorphism

\sigma:\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\to\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
as
\mathbf{Q}(q)
-algebras, by
f_{i}\mapsto f_{\sigma(i)}
.
We denote by
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}

the fixed point subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
by
\sigma
.

\sigma
induces a permutation on the canonical basis
\mathbf{B}
. We denote by

\mathbf{B}^{\sigma}
the set of
\sigma
-fixed elements in
\mathbf{B}
.
The notion of canonical signed bases is given as in [MSZ, 1.18].
Then
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}=\mathbf{B}\sqcup-\mathbf{B}
coincides with the canonical signed basis.
We denote by
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
the set of
\sigma
-fixed elements in
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}
.

1.2.

Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
. We denote by
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
Lusztig’s
integral form of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
, i.e.,
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
is the
\mathbf{A}
-subalgebra
of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
generated by
f_{i}^{(n)}=f_{i}^{n}/[n]^{!}_{d_{i}}
for
i\in I,n\in\mathbf{N}
,
where
d_{i}=(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})/2\in\mathbf{Z}_{>0}
.

\sigma
stabilizes
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
, and set

{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}=\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}\cap{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
, the
\mathbf{A}
-subalgebra
of
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
consisting of
\sigma
-fixed elements.

We assume that the order of
\sigma
is a power of a prime number
p
, and
let
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}
be the finite field of
p
-elements.
Set
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}=\mathbf{F}[q,q^{-1}]
, and consider an
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
-algebra
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
by

{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}=\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbf{A}}{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}\simeq{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}/p({}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}).

For each
x\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
, we denote by
O(x)
the orbit sum of
x
defined by

O(x)=\sum_{0\leq i<k}\sigma^{i}(x)
, where
k
is the smallest integer such that

\sigma^{k}(x)=x
.
Hence
O(x)\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}
.
O(x)
is similarly defined for
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.
Let
J
be the
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
-submodule of
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}
generated by

O(x)
for
x\in{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
such that
\sigma(x)\neq x
.
Then
J
is the two-sided ideal of
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}
. We define an

\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
-algebra
\mathbf{V}_{q}
by
\mathbf{V}_{q}={}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}/J
. Let

\pi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-\sigma}\to\mathbf{V}_{q}
be the natural projection.

1.3.

Let
\sigma:I\to I
be the diagram automorphism. We denote by

\underline{I}
the set of
\sigma
-orbits in
I
.

\sigma:I\to I
is called admissible, if for any
\eta\in\underline{I}
,

(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})=0
for all
i\neq j\in\eta
. The algebra
\mathbf{V}_{q}

was defined in [MSZ, 3.2] under the assumption that
\sigma
is admissible.
But, as is seen from 1.2, the definition of
\mathbf{V}_{q}
makes sense even
if
\sigma
is not admissible.

Assume that
\sigma
is admissible. Then by [MSZ, 2.1], one can construct
a Cartan datum
\underline{X}=(\underline{I},(\ ,\ )_{1})
induced from
(X,\sigma)
.
We denote by
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
the negative part of the quantized enveloping
algebra
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}
associated to
\underline{X}
.
The algebras
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}

and
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
are defined similarly to the case of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.

For each
\eta\in\underline{I},a\in\mathbf{N}
, set
\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}=\prod_{i}f_{i}^{(a)}
.
Since
\sigma
is admissible,
\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}
does not depend on the order of
the product, and
\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}\in{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}
. We denote its image
in
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}
also by
\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}
.
We define
g_{\eta}^{(a)}\in\mathbf{V}_{q}
by

(1.3.1)

g_{\eta}^{(a)}=\pi(\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}).

Note that
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
is generated by

\underline{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}=\underline{f}_{\eta}^{a}/[a]^{!}_{d_{\eta}}
for

\eta\in\underline{I}
and
a\in\mathbf{N}
. We denote by the same symbol
\underline{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}

its image in
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
.

The following result was proved in Theorem 3.4 and Theorem 4.18
(see also Remark 4.19)
in [MSZ]

Theorem 1.4
.

Assume that
\sigma
is admissible, and the order of
\sigma
is a power of
a prime number
p
. Then

(i)

The assignment
\underline{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}\mapsto g_{\eta}^{(a)}

gives an
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
-algebra isomorphism

\Phi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathbf{V}_{q}
.

(ii)

There exists the canonical signed basis
\widetilde{\underline{\mathbf{B}}}
of
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
,
and a natural bijection
\xi:\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\widetilde{\underline{\mathbf{B}}}
.

1.5.

We consider the case where
X
is finite or affine type.
Let
\varepsilon
be the order of
\sigma
.
Assume that
X
is irreducible, and
\sigma
is admissible.
In the case where
\varepsilon=2,3
,
Theorem 1.4 was proved by [SZ1, 2], by an elementary method.

In the remaining cases,
X
are affine, and
they are given as follows;

\displaystyle(A_{a}1)

\displaystyle\quad X

\displaystyle=D_{2n}^{(1)},

\displaystyle\quad\underline{X}

\displaystyle=A_{2n-2}^{(2)},\ (n\geq 3),

\displaystyle\quad\varepsilon

\displaystyle=4,

\displaystyle(A_{a}1^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad X

\displaystyle=D_{4}^{(1)},

\displaystyle\quad\underline{X}

\displaystyle=A_{2}^{(2)},

\displaystyle\quad\varepsilon

\displaystyle=4,

\displaystyle(A_{a}2)

\displaystyle\quad X

\displaystyle=A_{n-1}^{(1)},

\displaystyle\quad\underline{X}

\displaystyle=A_{m-1}^{(1)},\ (n=mc,1<c<n),

\displaystyle\quad\varepsilon

\displaystyle=c.

The diagrams are given as follows;

\displaystyle(A_{a}1)\quad X

\displaystyle=D_{2n}^{(1)}:

\displaystyle\underline{X}

\displaystyle=A_{2n-2}^{(2)}:

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern-3.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\underline{n}}$}}}}}}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle(A_{a}1^{\prime})\quad X

\displaystyle=D_{4}^{(1)}:

In the case (
A_{a}1
),

I=\{0,1,\dots,2n\}
and
\sigma
is given by

0\to 2n-1\to 1\mapsto 2n\mapsto 0
,

i\longleftrightarrow 2n-i
for
i=2,\dots,n
.
Thus
\varepsilon=4
, and

\underline{I}=\{\underline{0},\underline{i}\ (2\leq i\leq n-1),\underline{n}\}
.
In the case (
A_{a}1^{\prime}
),
I=\{1,2,2^{\prime},2^{\prime\prime},2^{\prime\prime\prime}\}
and
\sigma
is given by

\sigma:2\mapsto 2^{\prime}\mapsto 2^{\prime\prime}\mapsto 2^{\prime\prime\prime}\mapsto 2
, and

\sigma(1)=1
. Thus
\varepsilon=4
with

\underline{1}=\{1\}
and
\underline{2}=\{2,2^{\prime},2^{\prime\prime},2^{\prime\prime\prime}\}
.
In the case (
A_{a}2
), we identify
I=\{0,1,\dots,n-1\}
with

\mathbf{Z}/n\mathbf{Z}
. Then
\sigma
is given by
i\mapsto i+m
for
i\in\mathbf{Z}/n\mathbf{Z}
.
Thus
\varepsilon=n/m=c
, and
\underline{I}=\{\underline{0},\dots\underline{m-1}\}\simeq\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}
.

1.6.

Assume that
X
is of finite type. In [L2], Lusztig proved
the existence of a natural bijection
\mathbf{B}^{\sigma}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\mathbf{B}}

even in the case where
\sigma
is not admissible.
Thus one can expect that Theorem 1.4 can be generalized to the
case where
\sigma
is not admissible. In the remainder of this section,
we consider the case where
X
is finite or affine, and
\sigma
is
not admissible. Note that
X
is simply-laced, i.e.,

(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})\in\{0,-1\}
for
i\neq j
, and
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})=2
for any
i\in I
.
For each
\eta\in\underline{I}
, set
\delta_{\eta}=1
if
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})=0
for any

i\neq j
in
\eta
, and
\delta_{\eta}=2
otherwise.
Note that
\sigma
is admissible if and only if
\delta_{\eta}=1
for any
\eta\in\underline{I}
.
We define a symmetric bilinear form
(\ ,\ )_{1}
on the vector space

\bigoplus_{\eta\in\underline{I}}\mathbf{Q}\alpha_{\eta}
over
\mathbf{Q}
by

(1.6.1)

(\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{\eta^{\prime}})_{1}=\begin{cases}2\delta_{\eta}|\eta|&\quad\text{ if }\eta=\eta^{\prime},\\
-\delta_{\eta}\delta_{\eta^{\prime}}\sharp\{(i,j)\in\eta\times\eta^{\prime}\mid(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})\neq 0\}&\quad\text{ if }\eta\neq\eta^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Then
\underline{X}=(\underline{I},(\ ,\ ))
gives a Cartan datum since

(\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{\eta})_{1}\in 2\mathbf{Z}_{>0}
, and

\frac{2(\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{\eta^{\prime}})_{1}}{(\alpha_{\eta^{\prime}},\alpha_{\eta^{\prime}})_{1}}=\frac{1}{\delta_{\eta^{\prime}}|\eta^{\prime}|}\sum_{j\in\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta}\delta_{\eta^{\prime}}\sum_{i\in\eta}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})\in\mathbf{Z}_{\leq 0}.

1.7.

Assume that
\sigma
is non-admissible. If
X
is irreducible of finite type,
only the following case occurs;
X
is of type
A_{2n}
with

I=\{1,2,\dots,2n\}
, and
\sigma:i\leftrightarrow 2n-i+1
with
\varepsilon=2
.
Then
\underline{X}
is of type

C_{n}
with
\underline{I}=\{\underline{1},\dots,\underline{n}\}
.

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern-3.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern-3.5pt\raise-10.80011pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\underline{0}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 32.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 32.5pt\raise-10.80011pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\underline{1}}$}}}}}}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle(A_{n}5)\quad X

\displaystyle=A_{n-1}^{(1)}:

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 75.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern-3.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 25.60999pt\raise-46.80011pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-3.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{n-2}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 68.5pt\raise-36.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern \displaystyle\underline{X}=A_{1}:

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern-3.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern-3.5pt\raise-10.80011pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\underline{0}}$}}}}}}}}}\ignorespaces

The actions of
\sigma
on
I
are as follows.
In (
A_{n}1
),
\sigma:i\leftrightarrow 2n+1-i
for
0\leq i\leq 2n+1
.
In (
A_{n}2
),
\sigma:0\mapsto 2n\mapsto 1\mapsto 2n+1\mapsto 0
and

i\leftrightarrow 2n+1-i
for
2\leq i\leq 2n-1
.
In (
A_{n}3
),
\sigma:i\leftrightarrow 2n+1-i
for
1\leq i\leq 2n
, and

\sigma(0)=0
. In (
A_{n}4
),
\sigma:i\leftrightarrow 2n+1-i
for
0\leq i\leq 2n+1
.
In (
A_{n}2^{\prime}
),
\sigma:0\mapsto 4\mapsto 1\mapsto 5\mapsto 0
and

2\leftrightarrow 3
.
In (
A_{n}3^{\prime}
),
\sigma:1\leftrightarrow 2
and
\sigma(0)=0
.
In (
A_{n}4^{\prime}
),
\sigma:0\leftrightarrow 3,1\leftrightarrow 2
.
In (
A_{n}5
),
\sigma:0\mapsto 1\mapsto 2\mapsto\cdots\mapsto n-1\mapsto 0
.

Remarks 1.8.

(i) In [L2], Lusztig defines a Cartan datum
\underline{X}
in the non-admissible case,
by using the inner product which corresponds to

(\alpha_{\eta},\alpha_{\eta^{\prime}})_{1}/\delta
, where
\delta=\max\{\delta_{\eta},\delta_{\eta^{\prime}}\}
.
Although the Cartan matrix is the same, (1.6.1) is more convenient for our later discussion.

(ii) The numbering of
A_{2n}^{(2)}
obtained from
A_{2n+1}^{(1)}

by non-admissible
\sigma
(the cases (
A_{n}3
), (
A_{n}3^{\prime}
)) is reverse to the order obtained
from
D_{2n+1}^{(1)}
by admissible
\sigma
(see 5.1 (5), (6) in [SZ2]).
The numbering of (
A_{n}3
), (
A_{n}3^{\prime}
)) coincides with those in Kac’s textbook [K],
but is reverse to the one in Beck-Nakajiam [BN].

Remark 1.9.

In the case (
A_{n}5
), the
\sigma
-invariant canonical basis
does not exist. We follow the description in the lecture note by Schiffmann [S, 2.4].
Consider the cyclic quiver
\overrightarrow{Q}
associated to
X
, where
the orientation is given by

\sigma:0\mapsto 1\mapsto 2\mapsto\cdots\mapsto n-1\mapsto 0
.
Then
\sigma
gives an automorphism of the quiver
\overrightarrow{Q}
.
We identify
I
with
\mathbf{Z}/n\mathbf{Z}
. We denote by
I_{[i;m]}
(
i\in I,m\in\mathbf{N}
)
the unique nilpotent
indecomposable representation of
\overrightarrow{Q}
with socle
\varepsilon_{i}
and length
m
.
Let
\Pi^{n}
be the set of
n
-tuple of partitions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dots,\lambda^{(n)})
,
where
\lambda^{(i)}=(\lambda^{(i)}_{1}\geq\lambda^{(i)}_{2}\geq\cdots)
is a partition.
The set of isomorphism classes of nilpotent representations of
\overrightarrow{Q}

is identified with
\Pi^{n}
by the correspondence

\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dots,\lambda^{(n)})\mapsto M_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\bigoplus_{j}I_{[i;\lambda^{(i)}_{j}]}.

Let
\nu=\operatorname{Dim}M_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
, and consider the representation space

E(\overrightarrow{Q},\nu)
of
\overrightarrow{Q}
. We denote by

\mathscr{O}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
the nilpotent orbit in
E(\overrightarrow{Q},\nu)
corresponding to

M_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
. Let
\mathbf{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\operatorname{IC}(\overline{\mathscr{O}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{l})
be the simple
perverse sheaf (up to shift) on
E(\overrightarrow{Q},\nu)
corresponding to the orbit
\mathscr{O}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{\overrightarrow{Q}}
be the set of simple perverse sheaves as defined
in [S, 1.4]. The following result was proved by Lusztig [L3], [L4].

(1.9.1) Assume that
n>1
. Then

\mathcal{P}_{\overrightarrow{Q}}=\{\mathbf{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi^{n},\boldsymbol{\lambda}\text{ : aperiodic }\},

where
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(i)}_{j})\in\Pi^{n}
is called aperiodic if

\lambda^{(1)},\cdots,\lambda^{(n)}
have no common parts, namely for any
integer
c>0
, there exists
i
such that
c
does not appear
as
\lambda^{(i)}_{j}
.

\sigma
maps
M_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
onto
M_{\sigma(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}
, where

\sigma(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=(\lambda^{(2)},\lambda^{(3)},\dots,\lambda^{(n)},\lambda^{(1)})
. Hence

\sigma
acts on the set of
\{\mathbf{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\mid\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Pi^{n}\}
as a permutation,

\mathbf{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\mapsto\mathbf{P}_{\sigma(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}
.
In particular,
\mathbf{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is
\sigma
-invariant if and only if

\lambda^{(1)}=\lambda^{(2)}=\cdots=\lambda^{(n)}
, in which case
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is not aperiodic.
Thus (1.9.1) implies that

\sigma
acts on
\mathcal{P}_{\overrightarrow{Q}}
as a permutation, and there does not
exist
\sigma
-invariant element.
From the definition of canonical
basis, there exits a bijection
\mathcal{P}_{\overrightarrow{Q}}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathbf{B}
, which is
compatible with the action of
\sigma
.
It follows that
\mathbf{B}^{\sigma}=\emptyset
.
Since
\pi(\mathbf{B}^{\sigma})
gives a basis of
\mathbf{V}_{q}
, we conclude that
\mathbf{V}_{q}=0
.
Summing up the above discussion, we have

(1.9.2) In the case of (
A_{n}5
), an analogue of Theorem 1.4 does not hold.

1.10.

In the rest of this paper, we assume that
X
is irreducible
of finite or affine type, and by Remark 1.9, we exclude the case (
A_{n}5
).
Let
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
(resp.
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
) the quantum enveloping algebra
associated to
X
(resp.
\underline{X}
). We follow the notation in [SZ1, 2].
In particular, for each
i\in I
, let
T_{i}:\mathbf{U}_{q}\to\mathbf{U}_{q}

be the braid group action, and similarly consider
T_{\eta}:\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}\to\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}

for
\eta\in\underline{I}
.

Let
W
be the Weyl group associated to
X
.
Assume that
X
is of finite type, and
w_{0}
is the longest element in
W
.
Let
\mathbf{h}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{N})
be a sequence of
i\in I
such that

s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{N}}
is a reduced expression of
w_{0}
. For a given sequence
\mathbf{h}
,
the PBW-basis
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}=\{L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})\mid\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{N}^{N}\}
for
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}

is defined as in [SZ1, 1.7].
In the case where
X
is of affine type, let
\mathbf{h}=(\dots,i_{-1},i_{0},i_{1},\dots)

be a doubly infinite sequence of
i\in I
as defined in [BN, 3.1].
For a given
\mathbf{h}
, and an integer
p
, the PBW-basis

\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h},p}=\{L(\mathbf{c},p)\mid\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{C}\}
of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
is defined as in
[SZ2, 1.5], where
\mathscr{C}
is a certain parameter set.

2.
The algebra
\mathbf{V}_{q}

2.1.

In this section, we assume that
\sigma
is not admissible.
First we consider the simplest case, namely,
X=A_{2}
and
\underline{X}=C_{1}
,
hence
I=\{1,2\}
with
\sigma:1\leftrightarrow 2
and
\underline{I}=\{\underline{1}\}
.

\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
has two generators
f_{1},f_{2}
.
Set

(2.1.1)

\displaystyle f_{12}

\displaystyle=T_{1}(f_{2})=f_{2}f_{1}-qf_{1}f_{2},

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{12}

\displaystyle=T_{2}(f_{1})=f_{1}f_{2}-qf_{2}f_{1}.

The action of
\sigma
on
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
is given by
\sigma:f_{1}\leftrightarrow f_{2}
.
Thus
\sigma(f_{12})=f^{\prime}_{12}
.
For
\mathbf{h}=(1,2,1)
,
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}=(2,1,2)
, (see 1.10)

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}=\{L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})

\displaystyle=f_{1}^{(c_{1})}f_{12}^{(c_{2})}f_{2}^{(c_{3})}\mid\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})\in\mathbf{N}^{3}\}

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}}=\{L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=f_{2}^{(c_{1})}{f^{\prime}}_{12}^{(c_{2})}f_{1}^{(c_{3})}\mid\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})\in\mathbf{N}^{3}\}

give two kinds of PBW-bases of
\mathbf{U}^{-}_{q}
.
The following formulas are known.

(2.1.2)

\displaystyle f_{2}^{(\ell)}f_{1}^{(m)}f_{2}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}q^{(\ell-k)(m-k)}\begin{bmatrix}\ell-k+n\\
\ell-k\end{bmatrix}f_{1}^{(m-k)}f_{12}^{(k)}f_{2}^{(\ell-k+n)},

(2.1.3)

\displaystyle f_{1}^{(\ell)}f_{2}^{(m)}f_{1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}q^{(m-k)(n-k)}\begin{bmatrix}n-k+\ell\\
n-k\end{bmatrix}f_{1}^{(n-k+\ell)}f_{12}^{(k)}f_{2}^{(m-k)}.

By (2.1.2) and (2.1.3), we see that, if
m\geq\ell+n
,

f_{2}^{(\ell)}f_{1}^{(m)}f_{2}^{(n)}
is
the canonical basis corresponding to the PBW-basis

f_{1}^{(m-\ell)}f_{12}^{(\ell)}f_{2}^{(n)}
, and

f_{1}^{(\ell)}f_{2}^{(m)}f_{1}^{(n)}
is the canonical basis corresponding to
the PBW-basis
f_{1}^{(\ell)}f_{12}^{(n)}f_{2}^{(m-n)}
.
Thus

(2.1.4)

\mathbf{B}=\{f_{2}^{(\ell)}f_{1}^{(m)}f_{2}^{(n)}\mid m\geq\ell+n\}\cup\{f_{1}^{(\ell)}f_{2}^{(m)}f_{1}^{(n)}\mid m\geq\ell+n\}

gives the canonical basis of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
, and the overlapping occurs only
when
f_{2}^{(\ell)}f_{1}^{(m)}f_{2}^{(n)}=f_{1}^{(n)}f_{2}^{(m)}f_{1}^{(\ell)}

with
m=\ell+n
.
Note that
\sigma(f_{2}^{(\ell)}f_{1}^{(m)}f_{2}^{(n)})=f_{1}^{(\ell)}f_{2}^{(m)}f_{1}^{(n)}
.
Let
\mathbf{B}^{\sigma}
be the set of
\sigma
-invariant elements in
\mathbf{B}
.
It follows from the above discussion that

(2.1.5) We have

\sigma(f_{2}^{(\ell)}f_{1}^{(m)}f_{2}^{(m-\ell)})=f_{1}^{(\ell)}f_{2}^{(m)}f_{1}^{(m-\ell)}=f_{2}^{(m-\ell)}f_{1}^{(m)}f_{2}^{(\ell)}
for
\ell\leq m
.
In particular,

\mathbf{B}^{\sigma}=\{f_{2}^{(a)}f_{1}^{(2a)}f_{2}^{(a)}\mid a\in\mathbf{N}\}.

More generally, the following result is also obtained from the previous discussion.

(2.1.6) Assume that
\nu=-m(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})\in Q_{-}
. Then

\{f_{1}^{(\ell)}f_{12}^{(m-\ell)}f_{2}^{(\ell)}\mid 0\leq\ell\leq m\}

gives the PBW -basis, and

\{f_{2}^{(\ell)}f_{1}^{(m)}f_{2}^{(m-\ell)}\mid 0\leq\ell\leq m\}
gives
the canonical basis of
(\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-})_{\nu}
, under the correspondence

f_{1}^{(\ell)}f_{12}^{(m-\ell)}f_{2}^{(\ell)}\leftrightarrow f_{2}^{(\ell)}f_{1}^{(m)}f_{2}^{(m-\ell)}
.

Concerning the action of
\sigma
on PBW-basis, we have the following..

Lemma 2.2
.

Assume that
X
is of type
A_{2}
. Then

\sigma
-invariant PBW-bases do not exist (except the trivial one).
The PBW-basis corresponding to the
\sigma
-invariant canonical basis

f_{2}^{(a)}f_{1}^{(2a)}f_{2}^{(a)}
is given by
f_{1}^{(a)}f_{12}^{(a)}f_{2}^{(a)}

for
\mathbf{h}=(1,2)
, and by
f_{2}^{(a)}f^{\prime(a)}_{12}f_{1}^{(a)}
for

\mathbf{h}^{\prime}=(2,1)
.
We have, for
a\geq 1
,

\sigma(f_{1}^{(a)}f^{(a)}_{12}f_{2}^{(a)})=f_{2}^{(a)}f^{\prime(a)}_{12}f_{1}^{(a)}\neq f_{1}^{(a)}f^{(a)}_{12}f_{2}^{(a)}.

Proof.

The latter assertion is clear from the previous discussion.
We show that
\sigma
-invariant PBW-basis does not exist.
If a PBW-basis
x
is
\sigma
-invariant, then the corresponding canonical
basis is
\sigma
-invariant, hence has the form
f_{2}^{(a)}f_{1}^{(2a)}f_{2}^{(a)}

for some
a\in\mathbf{N}
. It follows that
x=f_{1}^{(a)}f_{12}^{(a)}f_{2}^{(a)}
,
and
y=\sigma(x)=f_{2}^{(a)}f^{\prime(a)}_{12}f_{1}^{(a)}
.
It is enough to show that
x\neq y
if
a\geq 1
.
By (2.1.2), one can write as

(2.2.1)

f_{2}^{(a)}f_{1}^{(2a)}f_{2}^{(a)}=f_{1}^{(a)}f_{12}^{(a)}f_{2}^{(a)}+\sum_{0\leq k<a}c_{k}f_{2}^{(k)}f_{1}^{(2a)}f_{2}^{(2a-k)}

with
c_{k}\in\mathbf{Q}(q)
, where
c_{k}\neq 0
for some
k
.
If
f_{1}^{(a)}f_{12}^{(a)}f_{2}^{(a)}
is
\sigma
-invariant,
then the sum in the right hand side of (2.2.1) is
\sigma
-invariant.
It follows by (2.1.5) that
c_{k}=c_{2a-k}
for any
k
.
But since
0\leq k<a
, we have
2a-k>a
, and
c_{2a-k}
does not appear
in this sum. This is absurd, and
f_{1}^{(a)}f_{12}^{(a)}f_{2}^{(a)}

is not
\sigma
-invariant.
The lemma is proved.
∎

2.3.

We define
\mathbf{V}_{q}={}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}/J
as in 1.2, and
let
\pi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}\to\mathbf{V}_{q}
be the natural projection.
Let
\mathbf{B}
be the canonical basis of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
(of type
A_{2}
). Then

\sigma
permutes
\mathbf{B}
, and
\mathbf{B}^{\sigma}
is given as in (2.1.5).
For each
a\geq 0
, set

(2.3.1)

g^{(a)}_{\underline{1}}=\pi(f_{2}^{(a)}f_{1}^{(2a)}f_{2}^{(a)})=\pi(f_{1}^{(a)}f_{2}^{(2a)}f_{1}^{(a)})\in\mathbf{V}_{q}

Then
\pi(\mathbf{B}^{\sigma})=\{g^{(a)}_{\underline{1}}\mid a\in\mathbf{N}\}
gives
an
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
-basis of
\mathbf{V}_{q}
.

We consider
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
associated to the Cartan datum
\underline{X}
of type

C_{1}
. The canonical basis of
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
is given by

\{\underline{f}^{(a)}_{\underline{1}}\mid a\in\mathbf{N}\}
, where

\underline{f}^{(a)}_{\underline{1}}=([a]^{!}_{4})^{-1}\underline{f}_{\underline{1}}^{a}
.
(Note that
d_{\underline{1}}=(\alpha_{\underline{1}},\alpha_{\underline{1}})_{1}/2=4
by
(1.6.1).)

{}_{\mathbf{A}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
is an
\mathbf{A}
-subalgebra of
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
generated by

\underline{f}^{(a)}_{\underline{1}}
(
a\in\mathbf{N}
).
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
-algebra
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
is defined
as before. We show the following.

Proposition 2.4
.

Assume that
X
is of type
A_{2}
. Then we have

(2.4.1)

[a]^{!}_{4}g_{\underline{1}}^{(a)}=g_{\underline{1}}^{a}.

The correspondence
\underline{f}_{\underline{1}}^{(a)}\mapsto g_{\underline{1}}^{(a)}

gives an isomorphism

\Phi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathbf{V}_{q}
of
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
-algebras.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{ch}\mathbf{F}=2
, in order to prove the proposition, it is enough to
show that

(2.4.2)

(f_{1}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{1})^{a}\equiv([a]^{!})^{4}f_{1}^{(a)}f_{2}^{(2a)}f_{1}^{(a)}\mod J.

For
a\geq 1
, we consider the following statement.

(2.4.3)

[a]^{4}f_{1}^{(a)}f_{2}^{(2a)}f_{1}^{(a)}\equiv(f_{1}^{(a-1)}f_{2}^{(2a-2)}f_{1}^{(a-1)})(f_{1}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{1})\mod J.

Note that if (2.4.3) holds, then (2.4.2) follows by induction.
We prove (2,4,3).
Let
Z
be the right hand side of (2.4.3).
By using the formula (2.1.3), we can express
Z
as a linear
combination of PBW-bases
f_{1}^{(a)}f_{12}^{(b)}f_{2}^{(c)}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{a-1}\bigl(A_{k}f_{1}^{(2a-1-k)}f_{12}^{(k+1)}f_{2}^{(2a-1-k)}+B_{k}f_{1}^{(2a-2-k)}f_{12}^{(k+2)}f_{2}^{(2a-2-k)}+C_{k}f_{1}^{(2a-k)}f_{12}^{(k)}f_{2}^{(2a-k)}\bigr),

where

\displaystyle A_{k}

\displaystyle=q^{(2a-2-k)(a-1-k)-k}\begin{bmatrix}2a-2-k\\
a-1-k\end{bmatrix}[2a-1-k]^{2}[k+1]

\displaystyle\qquad+q^{(2a-2-k)(a-1-k)+(2a-1-2k)}\begin{bmatrix}2a-2-k\\
a-1-k\end{bmatrix}[2a-1-k]^{2}[k+1][2a-2-k],

\displaystyle B_{k}

\displaystyle=q^{(2a-2-k)(a-1-k)-(2a-3-k)}\begin{bmatrix}2a-2-k\\
a-1-k\end{bmatrix}[2a-2-k][k+1][k+2]

\displaystyle\qquad+q^{(2a-2-k)(a-1-k)+2}\begin{bmatrix}2a-2-k\\
a-1-k\end{bmatrix}[2]^{-1}[2a-3-k][2a-2-k][k+1][k+2],

\displaystyle C_{k}

\displaystyle=q^{(2a-2-k)(a-1k)+(4a-2-4k)}\begin{bmatrix}2a-2-k\\
a-1-k\end{bmatrix}[2]^{-1}[2a-1-k]^{2}[2a-2-k]^{2}.

From this formula, we see that the coefficient of
f_{1}^{(2a-k)}f_{12}^{(k)}f_{2}^{(2a-k)}

in
Z
is equal to

(2.4.4)

\begin{cases}A_{a-1}+B_{a-2}&\quad\text{ if $k=a$, }\\
B_{a-1}&\quad\text{ if $k=a+1$, }\\
0&\quad\text{ if $k\geq a+2$.}\end{cases}

On the other hand, by using (2.1.6),
Z
can be expressed as

(2.4.5)

Z=\sum_{0\leq i\leq 2a}m_{i}f_{1}^{(i)}f_{2}^{(2a)}f_{1}^{(2a-i)}.

Note that since
Z
is
\sigma
-invariant, we see, by (2.1.5),
that
m_{i}=m_{2a-i}
for each
i
.
By using (2.1.3), each
f_{1}^{(i)}f_{2}^{(2a)}f_{1}^{(2a-i)}
is written
by the PBW -basis. Then the coefficient of
f_{1}^{(2a-k)}f_{12}^{(k)}f_{2}^{(2a-k)}

in
Z
is given as

(2.4.6)

\sum_{i=0}^{2a-k}m_{i}q^{(2a-k)(2a-k-i)}\begin{bmatrix}2a-k\\
2a-k-i\end{bmatrix}.

By comparing (2.4.4) with (2.4.6), we see that

m_{0}=\cdots=m_{a-2}=0
, and

m_{a-1}=B_{a-1},\qquad m_{a-1}q^{a}[a]+m_{a}=A_{a-1}+B_{a-2},

where

\displaystyle B_{a-1}

\displaystyle=q^{-a+2}[a-1][a][a+1]+q^{2}[2]^{-1}[a-2][a-1][a][a+1],

\displaystyle A_{a-1}+B_{a-2}

\displaystyle=(q^{-a+1}[a]^{3}+q[a]^{3}[a-1])+(q[a]^{3}[a-1]+q^{a+2}[2]^{-1}[a-1]^{2}[a]^{3}).

This implies that
m_{a}=[a]^{4}
.

Z
is
\sigma
-invariant, and in the expansion of
Z
in (2.4.5)
the only
\sigma
-invariant term is
f_{1}^{(a)}f_{2}^{(2a)}f_{1}^{(a)}
,
whose coefficient is equal to
m_{a}=[a]^{4}
.
It follows that
Z\equiv[a]^{4}f_{1}^{(a)}f_{2}^{(2a)}f_{1}^{(a)}\mod J
, and
(2.4.3) holds. The lemma is proved.
∎

2.5.

We now consider the general case, namely,
X
is irreducible of finite or affine type,
and
\sigma
is non-admissible. We exclude the case (
A_{n}5
).

\mathbf{V}_{q}
is defined as in 1.2, and we can consider

{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}
corresponding to
\underline{X}
.
But as observed in the case where

X=A_{2}
(Proposition 2.4), the definition of
\Phi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\to\mathbf{V}_{q}

for the admissible case in 1.3 is not applicable for the non-admissible case.
We modify the definition of
\Phi
as follows.
Note that if
X
is irreducible and
\delta_{\eta}=2
, then

\varepsilon=2
or 4, and
\eta=\{i,i^{\prime}\}
with
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i^{\prime}})=-1

(see 1.7).

For
\eta\in\underline{I}
, and
a\in\mathbf{N}
,
we define
\widetilde{f}_{\eta}\in{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
by

(2.5.1)

\widetilde{f}^{(a)}_{\eta}=\begin{cases}f^{(a)}_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2a)}f_{i}^{(a)}&\quad\text{ if $\delta_{\eta}=2$ and
$\eta=\{i,i^{\prime}\}$,}\\
\prod_{i\in\eta}f_{i}^{(a)}&\quad\text{ if $\delta_{\eta}=1$,}\end{cases}

and set

(2.5.2)

g_{\eta}^{(a)}=\pi(\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}).

Note that
f_{i}^{(a)}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2a)}f_{i}^{(a)}=f_{i^{\prime}}^{(a)}f_{i}^{(2a)}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(a)}

if
\delta_{\eta}=2
, and
\widetilde{f}^{(a)}_{\eta}
does not depend on the order
of the product if
\delta_{\eta}=1
.

We prove the following.

Theorem 2.6
.

Assume that
X
is irreducible of finite or affine type, and

\sigma
is not admissible.
We exclude the cases (
A_{n}2^{\prime}
), (
A_{n}3^{\prime}
), (
A_{n}4^{\prime}
), (
A_{n}5
) in 1.8.
Then the correspondence
\underline{f}^{(a)}_{\eta}\mapsto g_{\eta}^{(a)}

gives rise to an isomorphism
\Phi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathbf{V}_{q}
.

Remark 2.7.

The theorem does not hold for the case (
A_{n}5
) since

\mathbf{V}_{q}=\{0\}
by Remark 1.9.
It is likely that the theorem holds for the cases (
A_{n}2^{\prime}
)
\sim
(
A_{n}4^{\prime}
).
Actually, in those three cases, the verification of the fact that

\Phi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\to\mathbf{V}_{q}
is a homomorphism (Proposition 2.8)
becomes more complicated, and it is not yet done. If we can find
a combinatorial discussion as in [MSZ, 5], this might be possible.

The first step towards the proof of the theorem is the following.

Proposition 2.8
.

Under the assumption of Theorem 2.6,
the assignment
\underline{f}^{(a)}_{\eta}\mapsto g_{\eta}^{(a)}

gives rise to a homomorphism
\Phi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\to\mathbf{V}_{q}
.

2.9.

Proposition 2.8 will be proved in Section 4.
Here, assuming Proposition 2.8, we continue the discussion.
The algebra homomorphism
r:\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\to\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}

is defined by
r(f_{i})=f_{i}\otimes 1+1\otimes f_{i}
, with respect
to the twisted algebra structure of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
. The
symmetric bilinear form
(\ ,\ )
on
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
is defined by
using the property of
r
. The bilinear form
(\ ,\ )
is
non-degenerate. A similar result also hold for
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
(see [MSZ, 1.3]).
Let
\mathbf{F}(q)
be the field of rational functions of
q
with coefficients in
\mathbf{F}
,
which contains
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}=\mathbf{F}[q,q^{-1}]
.
Set
{}_{\mathbf{F}(q)}\mathbf{V}_{q}=\mathbf{F}(q)\otimes_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{V}_{q}
.
Then by [MSZ, 3.6], the bilinear form
(\ ,\ )
on
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
induces
a non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form
(\ ,\ )
on
{}_{\mathbf{F}(q)}\mathbf{V}_{q}
.
(Note that the discussion there holds even if
\sigma
is not admissible).
On the other hand, the bilinear form
(\ ,\ )
on
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}

induces a symmetric bilinear form on
{}_{\mathbf{F}(q)}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
. This
bilinear form is non-degenerate by [MSZ, Prop. 3.7].
We have the following proposition.

Proposition 2.10
.

(i)

For any
x,y\in{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
, we have

(\Phi(x),\Phi(y))=(x,y)
.

(ii)

The map
\Phi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\to\mathbf{V}_{q}
is injective.

Proof.

The proposition is proved in a similar way as in
[SZ2, Prop. 2.8], [MSZ, Prop. 3.8].
The map
\Phi
can be extended to a homomorphism

\widetilde{\Phi}:{}_{\mathbf{F}(q)}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\to{}_{\mathbf{F}(q)}\mathbf{V}_{q}
.
In order to prove (ii), it is enough to show that
\widetilde{\Phi}
is injective.
This follows from (i) since the bilinear form on
{}_{\mathbf{F}(q)}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}

is non-degenerate. We prove (i).
The
\mathbf{A}
-submodule
J_{1}
of
({}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\otimes{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-})^{\sigma}

is defined as in the proof of Proposition 3.8 in [MSZ].
Note that in our case,
\varepsilon=2
or 4,
J_{1}
coincides with the
\mathbf{A}
-submodule
generated by
x+\sigma(x)
such that
\sigma^{2}(x)=x,\sigma(x)\neq x
.
We note the
formula, for
\eta\in\underline{I}
,

(2.10.1)

r(\widetilde{f}_{\eta})\equiv\widetilde{f}_{\eta}\otimes 1+1\otimes\widetilde{f}_{\eta}\mod J_{1}.

In fact, (2.10.1) was proved in [MSZ] if
\delta_{\eta}=1
.
We verify this for
\eta
such that
\delta_{\eta}=2
.
Assume that
\eta=\{i,i^{\prime}\}
with
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i^{\prime}})=-1
.
We have

(2.10.2)

\displaystyle r(f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{i})

\displaystyle=[2]^{-1}(f_{i}\otimes 1+1\otimes f_{i})(f_{i^{\prime}}\otimes 1+1\otimes f_{i^{\prime}})^{2}(f_{i}\otimes 1+1\otimes f_{i})

\displaystyle=f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{i}\otimes 1+1\otimes f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{i}+Z,

where

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=q^{-1}(f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}\otimes f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i}+f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i}\otimes f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad+q[2](f_{i}^{(2)}\otimes f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}+f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}\otimes f_{i}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad+f_{i}\otimes f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}+f_{i}\otimes f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{i}+f_{i^{\prime}}\otimes f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i}

\displaystyle\quad+f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{i}\otimes f_{i}+f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}\otimes f_{i}+f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i}\otimes f_{i^{\prime}}.

The first two terms in
Z
are of the form
x+\sigma(x)
, thus they are
contained in
J_{1}
.
In the third term, by using the Serre relation

f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}+f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{i}=f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}
. we have

f_{i}\otimes f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}+f_{i}\otimes f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{i}=f_{i}\otimes f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}.

Thus the third term is contained in
J_{1}
. Similarly, the 4th term is contained in
J_{1}
.
Hence
Z\in J_{1}
, and (2.10.1) holds.
Then the proof of (2.8.2) and (2.8.7) in [SZ2] is done in a similar
way as in the proof of Proposition 3.8 in [MSZ].
As in [SZ2], [MSZ], the proof of the proposition is reduced to the computation of

(\widetilde{f}_{\eta},\widetilde{f}_{\eta^{\prime}})
.
If
\delta_{\mu}=\delta_{\mu^{\prime}}=1
,
(\widetilde{f}_{\eta},\widetilde{f}_{\eta^{\prime}})
was computed in
(3.8.1) in [MSZ]. If
\delta_{\eta}\neq\delta_{\eta^{\prime}}
,
(\widetilde{f}_{\eta},\widetilde{f}_{\eta^{\prime}})=0

since the weights are different. By the same reason,

(\widetilde{f}_{\eta},\widetilde{f}_{\eta^{\prime}})=0
if
\delta_{\eta}=\delta_{\eta^{\prime}}=2

and
\eta\neq\eta^{\prime}
. Hence we may assume that
\eta=\eta^{\prime}
with
\delta_{\eta}=2
.
We have

\displaystyle(\widetilde{f}_{\eta},\widetilde{f}_{\eta})=(f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{i},f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{i})=[2]^{-1}(r(f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{i}),f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}\otimes f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i}).

r(f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{i})
is computed in (2.10.2). By comparing the weights,
the last term is equal to

\displaystyle q^{-1}[2]^{-1}

\displaystyle(f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}\otimes f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i}+f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i}\otimes f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}},f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}}\otimes f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i})

\displaystyle=q^{-1}[2]^{-1}\bigl((f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}},f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}})(f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i},f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i})+(f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i},f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}})(f_{i}f_{i^{\prime}},f_{i^{\prime}}f_{i})\bigr)

\displaystyle=q^{-1}[2]^{-1}(q^{2}+1)(f_{i},f_{i})(f_{i^{\prime}},f_{i^{\prime}})(f_{i^{\prime}},f_{i^{\prime}})(f_{i},f_{i})

\displaystyle=(1-q^{2})^{-4}.

Thus we have

(\widetilde{f}_{\eta},\widetilde{f}_{\eta^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}(1-q^{2})^{-4}&\quad\text{ if }\eta=\eta^{\prime},\delta_{\eta}=2,\\
(1-q^{2})^{-|\eta|}&\quad\text{ if }\eta=\eta^{\prime},\delta_{\eta}=1,\\
0&\quad\text{ if }\eta\neq\eta^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Since
q_{\eta}=q^{d_{\eta}}=q^{4}
if
\delta_{\eta}=2
, and

q_{\eta}=q^{|\eta|}
if
\delta_{\eta}=1
by (1.6.1), by comparing with

(\underline{f}_{\eta},\underline{f}_{\eta^{\prime}})_{1}=\begin{cases}(1-q^{2}_{\eta})^{-1}&\quad\text{ if $\eta=\eta^{\prime}$ }\\
0&\quad\text{ if $\eta\neq\eta^{\prime}$ }\end{cases}

we obtain the result corresponding to (2.8.3) in [SZ2].
Now the assertion (i) follows from (2.8.3) as in [SZ2].
The proposition is proved.
∎

2.11.

For the surjectivity of
\Phi
, the discussion in [SZ2] or [MSZ]
cannot be applied directly. The main ingredient for the proof
is the modified PBW-bases. In the case where
X
is of finite type,

\sigma
acts on the set of modified PBW-bases as a permutation.
In this case, the surjectivity will be proved in Theorem 3.7,
in a similar way as in [SZ1]. While in the case where
X
is of affine type,
it is not certain whether
\sigma
acts on the set of modified PBW-bases.
In this case, we prove the surjectivity in Proposition 6.21, by applying the properties
of Kashiwara operators. The discussion is similar to [SZ2]. However,
in contrast to [SZ2], we construct Kashiwara operators by making use of
modified PBW-bases.

3.
Modified PBW-basis - finite case

3.1.

In this section, we assume that
X
is of finite type.
Let
W
(resp.
\underline{W}
) be the Weyl group of
X
(resp. of
\underline{X}
).
Let
\underline{\mathbf{h}}
be a sequence of
\underline{I}
with respect to
\underline{W}
.
Then there exists a sequence
\mathbf{h}
of
I
obtained from
\underline{\mathbf{h}}
, unique
up to the permutation of
i
and
j
such that
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})=0

(see [SZ1, 1.6]).
Let
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}
be the PBW-basis of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
and
\underline{\mathscr{X}}_{\underline{\mathbf{h}}}

the PBW-basis of
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
as in 1.10.
It is shown in [SZ1, Lemma 1.8], in the case where
\sigma
is admissible,
that
\sigma
induces a permutation on
the set
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}
,
and the set of
\sigma
-invariant PBW-basis
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\sigma}

corresponds bijectively to the set
\mathscr{X}_{\underline{\mathbf{h}}}
,
If
\sigma
is not admissible, this does not hold, since for
X=A_{2}
,
there does not exist
\sigma
-invariant PBW-basis (Lemma 2.2).
In order to extend Lemma 1.8 in [SZ1] to the non-admissible
case, we will modify the definition of PBW-basis.

3.2.

We recall the definition of PBW-bases in [SZ1]. Let

\mathbf{h}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{\nu})
be a sequence of
I
(called the reduced sequence)
corresponding
to a reduced expression
w_{0}=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{N}}
of
w_{0}\in W
,
where
N=l(w_{0})
.
Let
\varDelta\subset Q
be the set of roots, and
\varDelta^{+}\subset Q_{+}

the set of positive roots.
Then
\mathbf{h}
gives a total
order
\{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{N}\}
of
\varDelta^{+}
, where
\beta_{i}
is
defined by

\beta_{i_{k}}=s_{i_{1}}\dots s_{i_{k-1}}(\alpha_{i_{k}})
.
We define a root vector
f_{\beta_{k}}^{(c)}
corresponding to
\beta_{k}\in\varDelta^{+}

and
c\in\mathbf{N}
by

(3.2.1)

f_{\beta_{k}}^{(c)}=T_{i_{1}}\cdots T_{i_{k-1}}(f_{i_{k}}^{(c)}).

Note that
\beta_{i_{1}}=\alpha_{i_{1}}
and so
f_{\beta_{i_{1}}}^{(c)}=f_{i_{1}}^{(c)}
.
For
\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{N})\in\mathbf{N}^{N}
,
set
L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})=f_{\beta_{i_{1}}}^{(c_{1})}\cdots f_{\beta_{N}}^{(c_{N})}
.
Then
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}=\{L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})\mid\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{N}^{N}\}
gives a PBW-basis
of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
associated to
\mathbf{h}
.
For a sequence
\underline{\mathbf{h}}
of
\underline{I}
, the PBW-basis
\underline{\mathscr{X}}_{\underline{\mathbf{h}}}
of
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}

is defined similarly.

Let
\sigma:I\to I
be the diagram automorphism.
Here we assume that
\sigma
is not necessarily admissible.

\sigma
induces an automorphism

\sigma:W\to W
. Let
\underline{W}
be the Weyl group associated to
\underline{I}
.
For each
\eta\in\underline{I}
, we denote by
t_{\eta}\in W
the longest element
in the Weyl subgroup
W_{\eta}
of
W
generated by
\{s_{i}\mid i\in\eta\}
.
Then
t_{\eta}\in W^{\sigma}
,
and the correspondence
s_{\eta}\mapsto t_{\eta}
gives an isomorphism

\underline{W}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,W^{\sigma}
.

Let
\underline{\mathbf{h}}=(\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{\underline{N}})

be a reduced sequence of
\underline{I}
, where
\underline{N}=l(\underline{w}_{0})
.
For each
\eta\in\underline{I}
, we denote by
\mathbf{h}_{\eta}
a
reduced sequence of
W_{\eta}
. Hence

\mathbf{h}_{\eta}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{|\eta|})
for
\eta=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{|\eta|}\}

if
\delta_{\eta}=1
, and
\mathbf{h}_{\eta}=(i,j,i)
or
(j,i,j)
if
\eta=\{i,j\}

with
\delta_{\eta}=2
.
Then by a juxtaposition of
\mathbf{h}_{\eta}
for each
\eta\in\underline{I}
,
we obtain a reduced sequence
\mathbf{h}
of
I
, namely,

(3.2.2)

\mathbf{h}=(\underbrace{i_{1},\dots,i_{a}}_{\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{1}}},\underbrace{i_{a+1},\dots,i_{b}}_{\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{2}}},i_{b+1},\dots)

For each
\eta\in\underline{I}
, we define
\widetilde{T}_{\eta}
by

(3.2.3)

\widetilde{T}_{\eta}=\begin{cases}\prod_{i\in\eta}T_{i}&\quad\text{ if $\delta_{\eta}=1$,}\\
T_{i}T_{j}T_{i}=T_{j}T_{i}T_{j}&\quad\text{ if $\delta_{\eta}=2$ with
$\eta=\{i,j\}$.}\end{cases}

Then
\widetilde{T}_{\eta}
does not depend on the choice of the order in
\eta
.
Since
\sigma\circ T_{i}\circ\sigma^{-1}=T_{\sigma(i)}
,
\widetilde{T}_{\eta}
commutes
with
\sigma
.

Now assume that
\sigma
is admissible.
For each
\mathbf{h}
and
\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{N}^{N}
, set

(3.2.4)

\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{k})}=\prod_{i_{\ell}\in\eta_{k}}f^{(c_{\ell})}_{\beta_{\ell}},

where
\mathbf{c}_{k}
is a subsequence of
\mathbf{c}
consisting of

c_{\ell}
such that
i_{\ell}\in\eta_{k}
. Thus
we have
L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})=\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{1}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{1})}\cdots\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{\underline{N}}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{\underline{N}})}
.
Note that
\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{k})}
can be expressed as

(3.2.5)

\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{k})}=\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{k-1}}\bigl(\prod_{i_{\ell}\in\eta_{k}}f_{i_{\ell}}^{(c_{\ell})}\bigr).

It follows from (3.2.5) that

\sigma(\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{k})})=\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{k-1}}\bigl(\prod_{i_{\ell}\in\eta_{k}}f_{\sigma(i_{\ell})}^{(c_{\ell})}\bigr).

This implies that
\sigma(L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h}))=L(\sigma(\mathbf{c}),\mathbf{h})
, where
the action of
\sigma
on
\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{N}^{N}
is given, for each

\mathbf{c}_{k}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{m-1},a_{m})
by
\sigma(\mathbf{c}_{k})=(a_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{m-1})
.

For each
\underline{\mathbf{c}}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{\underline{N}})\in\mathbf{N}^{\underline{N}}
,
define
\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{N}^{N}
by

(3.2.6)

\mathbf{c}=(\underbrace{c_{1},\dots,c_{1}}_{\eta_{1}},\underbrace{c_{2},\dots,c_{2}}_{\eta_{2}},c_{3},\dots),

Then
L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})
is
\sigma
-invariant. Summing up the above discussion
we have

Lemma 3.3

([SZ1, Lemma 1.8])
.

Assume that
\sigma
is admissible. Then

\sigma
acts on
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}
, as a permutation,

\sigma:L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})\mapsto L(\sigma(\mathbf{c}),\mathbf{h})
.
Let
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\sigma}
be the set of
\sigma
-invariant
PBW-basis of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.
For each
\underline{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathbf{N}^{\underline{N}}
,
define
\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{N}^{N}
as in (3.2.6).
Then
L(\underline{\mathbf{c}},\underline{\mathbf{h}})\mapsto L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})

gives a bijection
\underline{\mathscr{X}}_{\underline{\mathbf{h}}}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\sigma}
.

3.4.

We now assume that
\sigma
is not admissible.
We modify the definition of
\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{k})}
as follows.
Assume that
\delta_{\eta}=2
with
\eta=\{i,j\}
. Let

\mathbf{h}_{\eta}=(i,j,i)
or
(j,i,j)
be a reduced sequence associated
to
W_{\eta}
. Let
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}_{\eta}}
be the set
of PBW-basis of the subalgebra
\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}
of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
isomorphic to

\mathbf{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}_{3})^{-}
.
For each PBW-basis
L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h}_{\eta})\in\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}_{\eta}}
in
\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}
,
the canonical basis
b(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h}_{\eta})\in\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}
can be assigned,
and
\mathbf{B}_{\eta}=\{b(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h}_{\eta})\mid\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{N}^{3}\}

gives the canonical basis of
\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}
.
We define
\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{k})}
by

(3.4.1)

\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{k})}=\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{k-1}}(b(\mathbf{c}_{k},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}})).

In the case where
\delta_{\eta}=1
, define
\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{k})}

by using (3.2.5). For any
\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{N}^{N}
, define

(3.4.2)

L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})=\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{1}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{1})}\cdots\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{\underline{N}}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{\underline{N}})},

and set
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\natural}=\{L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})\mid\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{N}^{N}\}
.
In the case where
\delta_{\eta}=2
,
\sigma
leaves
\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}
invariant, and
\sigma
permutes
the canonical basis
\mathbf{B}_{\eta}
. It follows that

\sigma(\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{k})})=\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{k})}
, where

\mathbf{c}_{k}^{\prime}
is determined by the condition that

\sigma(b(\mathbf{c}_{k},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}}))=b(\mathbf{c}_{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}})
.
From this, we see that
\sigma
acts on
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\natural}
as a permutation.
Note that, for a given
\underline{\mathbf{c}}
, the definition of
\mathbf{c}
in (3.2.6)
makes sense even for the non-admissible case (under the assignment

c\mapsto(c,c,c)
for the case
\delta_{\eta}=2
), and one can define

L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})
.

The following result is a generalization of Lemma 3.3 to the case
where
\sigma
is non-admissible.

Lemma 3.5
.

Assume that
\sigma
is not necessarily admissible.
Then
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\natural}
is a
\mathbf{Q}(q)
-basis of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
, and an

\mathbf{A}
-basis of
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.
\sigma
acts as a permutation
on
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\natural}
. The correspondence
L(\underline{\mathbf{c}},\underline{\mathbf{h}})\mapsto L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})

gives a bijection
\underline{\mathscr{X}}_{\underline{\mathbf{h}}}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,(\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\natural})^{\sigma}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\eta_{k}=\{i,j\}
with
\delta_{\eta_{k}}=2
,
and take
\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}}=(i,j,i)
. Then

b(\mathbf{c}_{k},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}})
is written as
b(\mathbf{c}_{k},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}})=L(\mathbf{c}_{k},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}})+z
,
where
z
is a
q\mathbf{Z}[q]
-linear combination of PBW-bases

L(\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{k},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}})
, with
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{k}>\mathbf{c}_{k}
(the lexicographic order on
\mathbf{N}^{3}

from the left).
Furthermore,

L(\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{k},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}})=f_{i}^{(c_{1}^{\prime})}T_{i}(f_{j}^{(c_{2}^{\prime})})T_{i}T_{j}(f_{i}^{(c_{3}^{\prime})})

for
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{k}=(c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime},c_{3}^{\prime})
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{k-1}}(L(\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{k},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}}))

\displaystyle=T_{i_{1}}\cdots T_{i_{\ell-3}}\bigl(f_{i_{\ell-2}}^{(c_{1}^{\prime})}T_{i_{\ell-2}}(f_{i_{\ell-1}}^{(c_{2}^{\prime})})T_{i_{\ell-2}}T_{i_{\ell-1}}(f_{i_{\ell}}^{(c_{3}^{\prime})})\bigr)

\displaystyle=f_{\beta_{\ell-2}}^{(c_{1}^{\prime})}f_{\beta_{\ell-1}}^{(c_{2}^{\prime})}f_{\beta_{\ell}}^{(c_{3}^{\prime})},

where we write
\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}}=(i_{\ell-2},i_{\ell-1},i_{\ell})
as a subsequence of
\mathbf{h}
.
Thus
\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{k})}
is a linear combination of

f_{\beta_{\ell-2}}^{(c_{1}^{\prime})}f_{\beta_{\ell-1}}^{(c_{2}^{\prime})}f_{\beta_{\ell}}^{(c_{3}^{\prime})}
for
various
(c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime},c_{3}^{\prime})
.
If
\eta_{k}
is such that
\delta_{\eta_{k}}=1
,
\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{k})}

is the same as in 3.2.
Hence
L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})
can be written as

L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})=L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})+z^{\prime}
, where
z^{\prime}
is a
q\mathbf{Z}[q]
-linear
combination of PBW-bases
L(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{h})
with
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}>\mathbf{c}
, the lexicographic
order on
\mathbf{N}^{N}
from the left.
Here the transition matrix between
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}
and
\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\mathbf{h}}
is
expressed as a triangular matrix
where the diagonal entries are 1, and off diagonals are contained
in
q\mathbf{Z}[q]
. It follows that
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\natural}
gives an
\mathbf{A}
-basis of

{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
, and an
\mathbf{Q}(q)
-basis of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.
We know already that
\sigma
acts on
\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\natural}
as a permutation.
From the discussion in 3.4, for
\eta_{k}
such that
\delta_{\eta_{k}}=2
,

\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{c}_{k})}
is
\sigma
-invariant if and only if
b(\mathbf{c}_{k},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}})

is
\sigma
-invariant. By Lemma 2.2, this condition is given by

c_{\mathbf{k}}=(c,c,c)
for some
c\in\mathbf{N}
.
Hence the condition that
L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})
is
\sigma
-invariant is given
by
\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{N}^{N}
obtained from
\underline{\mathbf{c}}
as in (3.2.6). The lemma is proved.
∎

3.6.

\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\mathbf{h}}=\{L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})\}
is called the modified PBW-basis associated to

\mathbf{h}
. Concerning the relation with the canonical basis,
the modified PBW-basis satisfies a similar property as
the PBW-basis. The proof is straightforward from the construction in 3.4.
Let
b(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})
be the canonical basis corresponding to
L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})
. Then

(3.6.1)

b(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})=L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})+\sum_{\mathbf{c}<\mathbf{d}}a_{\mathbf{d}}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{h}),

where
a_{\mathbf{d}}\in q\mathbf{Z}[q]
.
Note that the canonical bases are characterized by (3.6.1) and
by the property
\overline{b(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})}=b(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})
.

We consider the map
\pi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}\to\mathbf{V}_{q}
.
The image of
\mathbf{B}^{\sigma}
(resp.
(\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\natural})^{\sigma}
)
on
\mathbf{V}_{q}
gives an
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
-basis of
\mathbf{V}_{q}
, which we denote by the same symbol.
For
L(\underline{\mathbf{c}},\underline{\mathbf{h}})\in\underline{\mathscr{X}}_{\underline{\mathbf{h}}}
, we denote by
b(\underline{\mathbf{c}},\underline{\mathbf{h}})

the corresponding canonical basis in
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
.

In the case where
X
is of finite type, we can prove
Theorem 2.6, assuming Proposition 2.8, in a similar way
as in [SZ1].
The following result is a generalization of [SZ1, Thm. 1.14] to the non-admissible
case.

Theorem 3.7
.

Assume that
(X,\underline{X})=(A_{2n},C_{n})
, i.e.
(F_{n}1)
in 1.8.
Let
L(\underline{\mathbf{c}},\underline{\mathbf{h}})\mapsto L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})
be the bijection

\underline{\mathscr{X}}_{\underline{\mathbf{h}}}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,(\mathscr{X}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\natural})^{\sigma}
given in Lemma 3.5.
Let
\Phi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\to\mathbf{V}_{q}
be the homomorphism
given in Proposition 2.8. Then

(i)

The map
\Phi
gives an isomorphism
{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathbf{V}_{q}
.

(ii)

\Phi(L(\underline{\mathbf{c}},\underline{\mathbf{h}}))=L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})
.

(iii)

\Phi(b(\underline{\mathbf{c}},\underline{\mathbf{h}}))=b(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})
.

Proof.

We know already that
\Phi
is injective. The surjectivity of

\Phi
will follow from (ii). Thus (i) follows from (ii).
We prove (ii).
By (3.4.1) and (3.4.2), in order to prove (ii), it is enough to show that

(3.7.1)

\Phi\bigl(\underline{T}_{\eta_{1}}\cdots\underline{T}_{\eta_{k-1}}(\underline{f}_{\eta_{k}})\bigr)=\pi\bigl(\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{k-1}}(\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}})\bigr),

where
\underline{T}_{\eta}
denotes the braid group action on
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}
.
By a similar argument as in the proof of Theorem 1.14 in [SZ1],
the proof of (3.7.1) is reduced to the case where the rank of
\underline{X}
is 2.
Thus we nay assume that
X
is of type
A_{4}
and
\underline{X}
is of type
C_{2}
.
We use the notation
I=\{1,1^{\prime},2,2^{\prime}\}
with

\sigma:1\leftrightarrow 1^{\prime},2\leftrightarrow 2^{\prime}
,
\underline{I}=\{\underline{1},\underline{2}\}
.
By considering the

*
-action on
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
and
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
, we may assume that

\underline{\mathbf{h}}=(\underline{1},\underline{2},\underline{1},\underline{2})
(see [SZ1, 4.1]).
It is enough to check the following equalities,

(a)
\Phi(\underline{f}_{1})=\pi(\widetilde{f}_{1})
,

(b)
\Phi(\underline{T}_{\underline{1}}(\underline{f}_{\underline{2}}))=\pi(\widetilde{T}_{1}(\widetilde{f}_{2}))
,

(c)
\Phi(\underline{T}_{\underline{1}}\underline{T}_{\underline{2}}(\underline{f}_{\underline{1}}))=\pi(\widetilde{T}_{1}\widetilde{T}_{2}(\widetilde{f}_{1}))
,

(d)
\phi(\underline{T}_{\underline{1}}\underline{T}_{\underline{2}}\underline{T}_{\underline{1}}(\underline{f}_{\underline{2}}))=\pi(\widetilde{T}_{1}\widetilde{T}_{2}\widetilde{T}_{1}(\widetilde{f}_{2}))
.

In the discussion below, we use the notation in Section 4
for the description of PBW-basis.
Since
\underline{T}_{\underline{1}}\underline{T}_{\underline{2}}\underline{T}_{1}(\underline{f}_{\underline{2}})=\underline{f}_{\underline{2}}
,

\widetilde{T}_{1}\widetilde{T}_{2}\widetilde{T}_{1}(\widetilde{f}_{2})=\widetilde{f}_{2}
, (a) and (d) follows from
the definition of
\Phi
. We verify (b) and (c).
First we show (c). It is easy to see that

\underline{T}_{\underline{1}}\underline{T}_{\underline{2}}(\underline{f}_{\underline{1}})=\underline{f}_{2}\underline{f}_{\underline{1}}-q^{4}\underline{f}_{\underline{1}}\underline{f}_{\underline{2}}
.
Hence we have

\Phi\bigl(\underline{T}_{\underline{1}}\underline{T}_{\underline{2}}(\underline{f}_{\underline{1}})\bigr)=\pi(f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}-q^{4}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}).

On the other hand,

\widetilde{T}_{1}\widetilde{T}_{2}(\widetilde{f}_{1})=T_{1}T_{1^{\prime}}T_{2}T_{2^{\prime}}T_{2}(f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}})=f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}
.
By a similar computation as in Section 4, one can check that

f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}\equiv q^{4}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}\mod J.

Hence (c) holds. For (b), we have

\underline{T}_{\underline{1}}(\underline{f}_{\underline{2}})=\underline{f}_{\underline{2}}\underline{f}_{\underline{1}}^{(2)}-q^{2}\underline{f}_{\underline{1}}\underline{f}_{\underline{2}}\underline{f}_{\underline{1}}+q^{4}\underline{f}_{1}^{(2)}\underline{f}_{\underline{2}}
.
Hence

(3.7.2)

\displaystyle\Phi(\underline{T}_{\underline{1}}(\underline{f}_{\underline{2}}))=\pi(f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}-q^{2}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}+q^{4}f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}).

On the other hand, we have
T_{1}T_{1^{\prime}}(f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2})=f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}
.
By a direct computation, we have

\displaystyle f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\equiv q^{8}f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}+q^{2}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}\mod J,

\displaystyle f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\equiv f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}+q^{4}[2]^{2}f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}\mod J.

Substituting this into the right hand side of (3.7.2), we obtain the
equality (b). Thus (3.7.1) is verified, and (ii) follows.

Since the bar-involution is compatible with the map

\Phi
, (iii) follows from (ii) by using (3.6.1) and the corresponding formula for

\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
. The theorem is proved.
∎

4.
The proof of Proposition 2.8

4.1.

We assume that
X
is irreducible,
\sigma
is not admissible,
and that
(X,\underline{X})
satisfies the condition in Theorem 2.6.
For the proof of Proposition 2.8, we need to show,
for
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\underline{I}
, that

(4.1.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{1-a_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}}(-1)^{k}g_{\eta}^{(k)}g_{\eta^{\prime}}g_{\eta}^{(1-a_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}-k)}=0,\quad(\eta\neq\eta^{\prime}),

(4.1.2)

\displaystyle[a]^{!}_{|\eta|}g_{\eta}^{(a)}=g_{\eta}^{a},\quad(a\in\mathbf{N}).

(4.1.2) is known if
\delta_{\eta}=1
(see [SZ1, 3.1]),
and it was proved
in Proposition 2.4 if
\delta_{\eta}=2
.
If
\delta_{\eta}=\delta_{\eta^{\prime}}=1
, (4.1.1) was already verified in
[SZ1, 3.2]. Thus from the tables in 1.8, for the proof of (4.1.1),
it is enough to consider the case where
X=A_{4},\underline{X}=C_{2}

(note that we have excluded the cases (
A_{n}2^{\prime}
)
\sim
(
A_{n}4^{\prime}
) and (
A_{n}5
) in 1.8).
We write
I=\{1,2,2^{\prime},1^{\prime}\}

with
\sigma:1\leftrightarrow 1^{\prime},2\leftrightarrow 2^{\prime}
. Set
\underline{I}=\{\underline{1},\underline{2}\}

with
\delta_{\underline{1}}=1,\delta_{\underline{2}}=2
.
We have
(\alpha_{\underline{1}},\alpha_{\underline{1}})_{1}=4,(\alpha_{\underline{2}},\alpha_{\underline{2}})_{1}=8
.
The Cartan matrix
\underline{A}
is given by

\underline{A}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\
-2&2\end{pmatrix}.

Thus we have only to prove the following two formulas.

(4.1.3)

\displaystyle g_{\underline{1}}g_{\underline{2}}^{(2)}-g_{\underline{2}}g_{\underline{1}}g_{\underline{2}}+g_{\underline{2}}^{(2)}g_{\underline{1}}=0,

(4.1.4)

\displaystyle g_{\underline{2}}g_{\underline{1}}^{(3)}-g_{\underline{1}}g_{\underline{2}}g_{\underline{1}}^{(2)}+g_{\underline{1}}^{(2)}g_{\underline{2}}g_{\underline{1}}-g_{\underline{1}}^{(3)}g_{\underline{2}}=0.

4.2.

Let
W=S_{5}
be the Weyl group of type
A_{4}
,
and
w_{0}
the longest element
in
W
. Then
l(w_{0})=10
. We consider the following reduced sequence
\mathbf{h}
of
I
.

\mathbf{h}=(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{10})=(1,1^{\prime},2,2^{\prime},2,1,1^{\prime},2,2^{\prime},2).

Correspondingly, we have a total order of
\varDelta^{+}
as follows;

\varDelta^{+}=\{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{10}\}=\{1,1^{\prime},12,11^{\prime}22^{\prime},1^{\prime}2^{\prime},1^{\prime}22^{\prime},122^{\prime},2,22^{\prime},2^{\prime}\},

where
\beta_{k}=s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\cdots s_{i_{k-1}}(\alpha_{i_{k}})
.
We define
f_{\beta_{k}}^{(c)}=T_{i_{1}}\cdots T_{i_{k-1}}(f_{i_{k}}^{(c)})
,
and set
L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})=f_{\beta_{1}}^{(c_{1})}\cdots f_{\beta_{10}}^{(c_{10})}

for
\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{10})\in\mathbf{N}^{10}
.
Then
\{L(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h})\mid\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{N}^{10}\}
gives a PBW-basis of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.

f_{\beta_{1}},\dots,f_{\beta_{10}}
are written explicitly as follows;

\displaystyle f_{\beta_{1}}

\displaystyle=f_{1},\qquad f_{\beta_{2}}=f_{1^{\prime}},\qquad f_{\beta_{10}}=f_{2^{\prime}},\qquad f_{\beta_{8}}=f_{2},\qquad f_{\beta_{3}}=f_{2}f_{1}-qf_{1}f_{2},

\displaystyle f_{\beta_{5}}

\displaystyle=f_{2^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}}-qf_{1^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}},\qquad f_{\beta_{9}}=f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}-qf_{2}f_{2^{\prime}},\qquad f_{\beta_{7}}=f_{22^{\prime}}f_{1}-qf_{1}f_{22^{\prime}},

\displaystyle f_{\beta_{6}}

\displaystyle=f_{2}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}-qf_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{2},\qquad f_{\beta_{4}}=f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{12}-qf_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}.

(4.1.3) and (4.1.4) are equivalent to the formulas

(4.2.1)

\displaystyle(f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}})f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(4)}f_{2}^{(2)}-f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}(f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}})f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(4)}f_{2}^{(2)}(f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}})\equiv 0\mod J,

(4.2.2)

\displaystyle(f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2})f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}-f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}(f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2})f^{(2)}_{1}f^{(2)}_{1^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}(f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2})f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}-f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}(f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2})\equiv 0\mod J.

We shall prove (4.2.1) and (4.2.2) by expressing them in terms of
modified PBW-bases. We compute them by making use of various commutation relations
for
f_{\beta_{k}}
.

4.3.

We prove (4.2.2).
First we compute

\displaystyle f_{1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}(f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2})f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=q^{2}[3]\bigl(f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}+f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+q\bigl(f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+q\bigl(f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+q^{3}[3]\bigl(f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{12}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{12}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+q^{4}[3]^{2}f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}.

The first three terms in the right hand side are of the form

x+\sigma(x)
, hence contained in
J
.
Using the relation

f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}-f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}+f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}^{(2)}=0
,
the fourth term can be rewritten as

q^{3}[3](f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{12}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}+f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2})
, hence again contained in
J
.
It follows that

(4.3.1)

\begin{split}f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}&(f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2})f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}\\
&\equiv q^{4}[3]^{2}f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}\mod J.\end{split}

Next we compute

\displaystyle f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle f_{2}f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=q^{6}(f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{12}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}+f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{8}[2](f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{12}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}+f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{8}(f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}+f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{5}(f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{2}(f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{5}(f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{9}(f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{12}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{12}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{3}(f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}f_{2}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{2^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{12}f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+q^{4}f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}+f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}.

The first 6 terms in the right hand side
are of the form
x+\sigma(x)
, hence are contained in
J
.
The 7th term is contained in
J
by the same reason as the previous computation.
By applying
T_{1}T_{1^{\prime}}
to the relation

f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}=f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}^{(2)}+f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}
, we obtain

f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}=f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}+f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}
.
Thus, by a similar reason, the 8th term is also contained in
J
.
It follows that

(4.3.2)

\begin{split}f_{2}&f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}\\
&\equiv q^{12}f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+q^{4}f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}+f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}\mod J.\end{split}

Finally we compute

\displaystyle f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}(

\displaystyle f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2})f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=q^{3}[2]^{2}(f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{5}[2][3](f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}+f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{3}[2]^{2}(f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{4}[3](f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{12}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}+f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{6}[2][3](f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{12}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}+f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad+q[2](f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{6}[2][3](f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{12}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{12}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}+f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{2}[2](f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}f_{2}+f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{2^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{8}[3]^{2}f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+q^{2}[2]^{2}f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}+f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}.

The first 6 terms in the right hand side are of the form
x+\sigma(x)
, hence
are contained in
J
. The 7th and 8th terms are contained in
J
by the
same reason as before. It follows that

(4.3.3)

\begin{split}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}(f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}&f_{2})f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}\equiv q^{8}[3]^{2}f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}\\
&+q^{2}[2]^{2}f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}+f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}\mod J.\end{split}

Now putting together (4.3.1), (4.3.2) and (4.3.3), we see that
the left hand side of (4.2.2) is equal to

-F_{3}(q^{2})f_{1}^{(3)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+F_{2}(q^{2})f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}-F_{1}(q^{2})f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{12}\mod J,

where, for
a\geq 1
,
F_{a}(q)
is a polynomial in
q
defined by

F_{a}(q)=\sum_{t=0}^{a}(-1)^{t}q^{t(a-t)}\begin{bmatrix}a\\
t\end{bmatrix}.

Since
F_{a}(q)
is identically zero by [L1, 1.3.4 (a)],
(4.2.2) holds.

4.4

We prove (4.2.1).
We compute

\displaystyle f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=q^{2}(f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}+f_{1^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad+q(f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2}+f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad+q(f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}+f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{3}(f_{1^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}+f_{1^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}f_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{4}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}.

(Actually, this is a part of the computation of

f_{1}^{(2)}f_{1^{\prime}}^{(2)}(f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2})f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}

in 4.3.) Since the first three terms are written as
x+\sigma(x)
, they are
contained in
J
. The 4th term is contained in
J
by a similar discussion
as in 4.3. It follows that

(4.4.1)

f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}\equiv q^{4}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}+f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(4)}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=q^{3}(f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}+f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{4}(f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}^{(2)}+f_{1^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{2}(f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}+f_{12}f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}^{(2)}+f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{6}(f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}2^{\prime}}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}^{(2)}+f_{1^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(4)}f_{2}+f_{1^{\prime}}f_{12}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(4)}f_{2}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{8}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(4)}f_{2}^{(2)}+f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}.

The first two terms are written as
x+\sigma(x)
, hence they are contained
in
J
. For the third term and the 4th term, we note the relations

(4.4.2)

\displaystyle f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}+f_{2^{\prime}}^{(3)}f_{2}=f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}},

(4.4.3)

\displaystyle f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(4)}f_{2}^{(2)}+f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(4)}f_{2}=f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}^{(3)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}.

(4.4.2) and (4.4.3) are verified by using (2.1.2) and (2.1.3).
Hence the third and the 4th terms are also contained in
J
. It follows that

(4.4.4)

f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(4)}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}\equiv q^{8}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(4)}f_{2}^{(2)}+f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}\mod J.

By multiplying
f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}
on (4.4.1) from the right, we have

\displaystyle f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}(f_{1}f_{1}^{\prime})f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv q^{4}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}(f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2})^{2}+f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv q^{4}[2]^{4}f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(4)}f_{2}^{(2)}+f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}\mod J.

The second identity follows from (2.4.2) for
a=2
.
Combining this formula with (4.4.4), we see that the left hand side
of (4.2.1) is equal to

F_{2}(q^{4})f_{1}f_{1^{\prime}}f_{2}^{(2)}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(4)}f_{2}^{(2)}+F_{1}(q^{4})f_{1^{\prime}22^{\prime}}f_{122^{\prime}}f_{2}f_{2^{\prime}}^{(2)}f_{2}\equiv 0\mod J.

Hence (4.2.1) holds. Proposition 2.8 is proved.

5.
\sigma
-action on root systems

5.1.

In this section, we assume that
X
is irreducible of affine type,
and consider the pair
(X,\underline{X})
for
\sigma
: admissible
or non-admissible (but exclude the case (
A_{n}5
)).
Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the affine Kac-Moody algebra associated to
X
.
We write
I=\{0,1,\dots,n\}
, and set
I_{0}=I-\{0\}
, where

I_{0}
corresponds to the subalgebra
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
of
\mathfrak{g}
of finite type.
Recall that
Q=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{Z}\alpha_{i}
and let
\varDelta\subset Q
be the affine root
system for
\mathfrak{g}
.
Since
\sigma:I\to I
,
\sigma
acts on
Q
by
\alpha_{i}\mapsto\alpha_{\sigma(i)}
.

\varDelta
is decomposed as
\varDelta=\varDelta^{\operatorname{re}}\sqcup\varDelta^{\operatorname{im}}
, where

\varDelta^{\operatorname{re}}
is the set of real roots, and
\varDelta^{\operatorname{im}}=(\mathbf{Z}-\{0\})\delta
is the
set of imaginary roots.

\delta
is written as
\delta=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\alpha_{i}
. From the explicit table
of coefficients
a_{i}
(see [K]), we see
a_{i}
is symmetric with respect to
\sigma
.
Thus
\delta
is stable by
\sigma
