. On the other hand,
\varDelta^{\operatorname{re}}=W\Pi
, where

\Pi=\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I\}
is the set of simple roots.
Since
\Pi
is
\sigma
-stable, and
W
is
\sigma
-stable,
we see that
\varDelta^{\operatorname{re}}
is
\sigma
-stable. Hence the action of
\sigma
on
Q
leaves

\varDelta
invariant.
Let
\varDelta_{0}
be the subsystem of
\varDelta
corresponding to
I_{0}
.

5.2.

Let
\eta_{0}\subset I
be the
\sigma
-orbit containing 0. Set
I_{0}^{\prime}=I-\eta_{0}

and let
\varDelta_{0}^{\prime}
be the subsystem of
\varDelta_{0}
obtained from
I_{0}^{\prime}
. Then

I_{0}^{\prime}
and
\varDelta_{0}^{\prime}
are
\sigma
-stable.
We have
\underline{I}=\underline{I}_{0}^{\prime}\sqcup\{\eta_{0}\}
, and
\underline{I}_{0}^{\prime}
corresponds to
the Cartan datum
\underline{X}_{0}
of finite type.

For each
\beta\in\varDelta
, let
\mathscr{O}_{\beta}
be the
\sigma
-orbit of
\beta
in
\varDelta
.
If
\mathscr{O}_{\beta}=\{\beta,\sigma(\beta)\}
with
\beta+\sigma(\beta)\in\varDelta^{\operatorname{re}}
,
set
O(\beta)=O(\sigma(\beta))=O(\beta+\sigma(\beta))=2(\beta+\sigma(\beta))

(this only occurs in the case where
\sigma
is non-admissible).
Otherwise,
set
O(\beta)=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathscr{O}_{\beta}}\gamma
.
Let
Q^{\sigma}
be the set of
\sigma
-fixed elements in
Q
, and

(Q^{\sigma})^{\prime}
the
\mathbf{Z}
-submodule of
Q^{\sigma}
spanned by
\widetilde{\alpha}_{\eta}

with
\eta\in\underline{I}
, where

\widetilde{\alpha}_{\eta}=O(\alpha_{i})
for
i\in\eta
.
Recall that
\underline{Q}=\bigoplus_{\eta\in\underline{I}}\mathbf{Z}\underline{\alpha}_{\eta}
.
The map
\widetilde{\alpha}_{\eta}\mapsto\underline{\alpha}_{\eta}
gives an isomorphism

(Q^{\sigma})^{\prime}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{Q}
. This map is compatible with the inner product

(\ ,\ )
on
Q
and the inner product
(\ ,\ )_{1}
on
\underline{Q}
defined in (1.6.1).

Set
\underline{\varDelta}_{0}=\{O(\beta)\mid\beta\in\varDelta_{0}^{\prime}\}
,
which we consider as a subset of
\underline{Q}
under the identification

(Q^{\sigma})^{\prime}\simeq\underline{Q}
. Then it is easy to see that

\underline{\varDelta}_{0}
gives a root system of type
\underline{X}_{0}
with
simple system
\{O_{\eta}\mid\eta\in\underline{I}_{0}^{\prime}\}
, where

O_{\eta}=O(\alpha_{i})
for
i\in\eta
.

5.3.

Hereafter we assume that
X
is irreducible and simply-laced, hence it is of
untwisted type.
Set
\varSigma_{0}^{+}=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)\in\mathbf{Z}\delta\}
.
We shall determine the set
\varSigma_{0}^{+}
for each
(X,\underline{X})
.
If
\sigma
leaves
\varDelta_{0}
invariant, namely,
\eta_{0}=\{0\}
,
then
\varDelta_{0}=\varDelta_{0}^{\prime}
, and in this case,

\varSigma_{0}^{+}=\emptyset
. Thus we assume that
\varDelta_{0}^{\prime}\neq\varDelta_{0}
. In the case
where
\sigma
is admissible, and
\varepsilon\neq 4
,
\varSigma_{0}^{+}
is already determined
in [SZ2]. In the remaining cases
(X,\underline{X})
are given as follows;

(A)
(D_{2n}^{(1)},A_{2n-2}^{(2)}),\qquad\!\!(n\geq 2,\text{ $\sigma$ : admissible, $\varepsilon=4$})
,

(B)
(D_{2n+1}^{(1)},C_{n-1}^{(1)}),\quad\ (n\geq 2,\text{ $\sigma$ : non-admissible, $\varepsilon=4$,
$\underline{X}=A_{1}^{(1)}$ if $n=2$})
,

(C)
(D_{2n+1}^{(1)},A_{2n-1}^{(2)}),\quad(n\geq 2,\text{ $\sigma$ : non-admissible, $\varepsilon=2$})
,

(D)
(A_{2n+1}^{(1)},C_{n}^{(1)}),\qquad\!(n\geq 1,\text{ $\sigma$ : non-admissible, $\varepsilon=2$,
$\underline{X}=A_{1}^{(1)}$ if $n=1$})
.

5.4.

First consider the case (A), (B). Let
X=D_{m}^{(1)}
, where

m=2n
in the case (A), and
m=2n+1
in the case (B).
Set
I=\{0,1,\dots,m\}
, then
\sigma:I\to I
is given by

\sigma:0\mapsto m-1\mapsto 1\mapsto m\mapsto 0
,

i\leftrightarrow m-i
for
2\leq i\leq m-2
.
\eta_{0}=\{0,1,m-1,m\}

and
I^{\prime}_{0}=\{2,\dots,m-2\}
.
\varDelta_{0}
is of type
D_{m}
, and

\varDelta_{0}^{\prime}
is of type
A_{m-3}
. The highest root
\theta\in\varDelta^{+}_{0}

is given by
\theta=2\alpha_{1}+\cdots+2\alpha_{m-2}+\alpha_{m-1}+\alpha_{m}
.
Write
\alpha=\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}c_{i}\alpha_{i}
, and

z=\sum_{2\leq i\leq m-2}c_{i}\alpha_{i}
. We have

(5.4.1)

\displaystyle\sigma(\alpha)

\displaystyle=c_{1}\alpha_{m}+c_{m-1}\alpha_{1}+c_{m}(\delta-\theta)+\sigma(z)

(5.4.2)

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=c_{1}(\delta-\theta)+c_{m-1}\alpha_{m}+c_{m}\alpha_{m-1}+z,

since
\sigma^{2}
acts trivially on
\varDelta_{0}^{\prime}
.

For
i=1,2,4
, set
Z_{i}=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}\mid|\mathscr{O}_{\alpha}|=i\}
.
First assume that
\alpha\in Z_{1}
. Then
c_{1}=c_{m}=0
, and
so
\alpha=z\in\varDelta_{0}^{\prime}
. If we write

I_{0}^{\prime}=\{\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3},\dots,\varepsilon_{m-2}-\varepsilon_{m-1}\}
, then

Z_{1}=\begin{cases}\{\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{m-1},\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{m-2},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}-\varepsilon_{n+1}\}&\quad\text{if $m=2n$,}\\
\{\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{m-1},\varepsilon_{3}-\varepsilon_{m-2},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}-\varepsilon_{n+2}\}&\quad\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces if $m=2n+1$,}\crcr}}}}\end{cases}}

Note that
Z_{1}\cap\varSigma_{0}^{+}=\emptyset
.

Next assume that
\alpha\in Z_{2}
.
From (5.4.2), the condition
\alpha\in Z_{2}
is given by
c_{1}=0
and

c_{m}=c_{m-1}
. If we write
\varDelta_{0}=\{\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq m\}
,
this condition implies that

Z_{2}=\{\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}\mid 2\leq i<j\leq m-1\}-Z_{1}.

The action of
\sigma
on
Z_{2}
is given as follows;

(5.4.3)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\varepsilon_{m+1-j}-\varepsilon_{m+1-i},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\delta-(\varepsilon_{m+1-j}+\varepsilon_{m+1-i}).

From this, we see that

(5.4.4)

Z_{2}\cap\varSigma_{0}^{+}=\begin{cases}\{\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{m-1},\varepsilon_{3}+\varepsilon_{m-2},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}+\varepsilon_{n+1}\}&\quad\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces if $m=2n$, }\crcr}}}}\\
\{\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{m-1},\varepsilon_{3}+\varepsilon_{m-2},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}+\varepsilon_{n+2}\}&\quad\text{ if $m=2n+1$.}\end{cases}}

Next consider
Z_{4}
.
From the above discussion, we have

(5.4.5)

Z_{4}=\{\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}\mid i=1\text{ or }j=m\}.

We shall determine
Z_{4}\cap\varDelta_{0}^{+}
.
We consider the formula (5.4.1). Since
\alpha\in Z_{4}
, we have

c_{1}=1
or
(c_{m},c_{m-1})=(1,0),(0,1)
.
In the expression of (5.4.1), we only consider the first part,

x=c_{1}\alpha_{1}+c_{m-1}\alpha_{m-1}+c_{m}\alpha_{m}
.
Then

x+\sigma(x)+\sigma^{2}(x)+\sigma^{3}(x)=(c_{1}+c_{m-1}+c_{m})(\delta-\theta)+z^{\prime},

where
z^{\prime}
is a sum of elements in
\varDelta_{0}
.

Hence if
3\delta
appears in this sum, then
(c_{1},c_{m-1},c_{m})=(1,1,1)
.
If
2\delta
appears, then
(c_{1},c_{m-1},c_{m})=(1,1,0)
or
(1,0,1)
.
If
\delta
appears, then
(c_{1},c_{m-1},c_{m})=(1,0,0),(0,1,0),(0,0,1)
.
Write
\alpha=x+z
, where
x
and
z
are as above.
Then we have

O(\alpha)=(c_{1}+c_{m-1}+c_{m})O(\alpha_{0})+2(z+\sigma(z)).

Note that
O(\alpha_{0})=\delta-2(\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{m-1})
.
Set
\varSigma^{+}_{0}(k)=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)=k\delta\}
.
The condition that
\alpha\in\varSigma_{0}^{+}(k)
is given by

(5.4.6)

z+\sigma(z)=k(\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{m-1})=k(\sum_{2\leq i\leq m-2}\alpha_{i}).

Since

z+\sigma(z)=\sum_{2\leq i\leq m-2}(c_{i}+c_{m-i})\alpha_{i}
,
(5.4.6) implies a relation

(5.4.7)

c_{i}+c_{m-i}=k,\qquad(2\leq i\leq m-2).

Note that in the case where
m=2n
,
k=2c_{n}
.
Since
1\leq k\leq 3
, we must have
k=2
.
Thus
(c_{1},c_{2n-1},c_{2n})=(1,1,0)
or
(1,0,1)
.
Summing up the above discussion, we have the following.

Case (A) :
(X,\underline{X})=(D_{2n}^{(1)},A_{2n-2}^{(2)})
.

(5.4.8)

\displaystyle Z_{4}\cap\varDelta_{0}^{+}=\{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2n},\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2n}\}.

Combining (5.4.8) with (5.4.4), we have

(5.4.9)

\varSigma_{0}^{+}=\{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2n},\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2n},\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{2n-1},\varepsilon_{3}+\varepsilon_{2n-2},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}+\varepsilon_{n+1}\}.

Hence
\varSigma_{0}^{+}
is a positive subsystem of
\varDelta_{0}^{+}
of type
(n+1)A_{1}
.

Case (B) :
(X,\underline{X})=(D_{2n+1}^{(1)},C_{n}^{(1)})

By using (5.4.7), one can determine
Z_{4}\cap\varSigma_{0}^{+}(k)

as follows.

(5.4.10)

\displaystyle Z_{4}\cap\varDelta_{0}^{+}(3)

\displaystyle=\{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{n+1}\},

\displaystyle Z_{4}\cap\varDelta_{0}^{+}(2)

\displaystyle=\{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2n+1},\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2n+1}\},

\displaystyle Z_{4}\cap\varDelta_{0}^{+}(1)

\displaystyle=\{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{n+1},\varepsilon_{n+1}-\varepsilon_{2n+1},\varepsilon_{n+1}+\varepsilon_{2n+1}\}.

It follows that

(5.4.11)

\begin{split}\varSigma_{0}^{+}&=\{\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{2n},\varepsilon_{3}+\varepsilon_{2n-1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}+\varepsilon_{n+2}\}\\
&\quad\sqcup\{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{n+1},\varepsilon_{n+1}-\varepsilon_{2n+1},\varepsilon_{n+1}+\varepsilon_{2n+1},\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2n+1},\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2n+1},\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{n+1}\}.\end{split}

Hence
\varDelta_{0}^{+}
is a positive subsystem of
\varDelta_{0}^{+}
of type
(n-1)A_{1}+A_{3}
.

5.5.

Next consider the case (C) :
(X,\underline{X})=(D_{2n+1}^{(1)},A_{2n-1}^{(2)})
.

\varDelta_{0}
: type
D_{2n+1}
, where

\sigma:\alpha_{0}\leftrightarrow\alpha_{2n+1},\alpha_{i}\leftrightarrow\alpha_{2n+1-i}
for
i=2,\dots,2n-1
.
In this case,
\eta_{0}=\{0,2n+1\}
,
I_{0}^{\prime}=\{1,2,\dots,2n\}
,
and
\varDelta_{0}^{\prime}
is of type
A_{2n}
.
Here
\theta=\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}+\cdots+2\alpha_{2n-1}+\alpha_{2n}+\alpha_{2n+1}
.
We write
\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}
as

\alpha=\sum_{i\in I_{0}}c_{i}\alpha_{i}
, with
\eta_{0}=\{0,i_{1}\}
.
By the discussion in [SZ2, (5.3.1)], we see that

\varSigma_{0}^{+}=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)=\delta\}
, and the condition

\alpha\in\varSigma_{0}^{+}
is given by

(5.5.1)

c_{i}+c_{\sigma(i)}=a_{i},\qquad(i\in I_{0}^{\prime})

where we write
\delta=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\alpha_{i}
.
Here
\underline{\varDelta}_{0}
is of type
C_{n}
with
\alpha_{\underline{n}}
long root.
We have
c_{2n+1}=1
and

c_{i}+c_{\sigma(i)}=2,\quad(2\leq i\leq 2n-1),\qquad c_{1}+c_{2n}=1.

This implies that

(5.5.2)

\varSigma_{0}^{+}=\{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2n+1},\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{2n},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}+\varepsilon_{n+2}\}.

Hence
\varSigma_{0}^{+}
is a positive subsystem of
\varDelta^{+}_{0}
of type
nA_{1}
.

5.6.

Finally, consider the case (D) :
(X,\underline{X})=(A_{2n+1}^{(1)},C_{n}^{(1)})
.
Here
I=\{0,1,\dots,2n+1\}
with
\sigma:i\leftrightarrow 2n+1-i
.
Hence
\underline{I}=\{\underline{0},\underline{1},\dots,\underline{n}\}
.

I_{0}^{\prime}=\{1,2,\dots,2n\}
and
\varDelta_{0}^{\prime}
is of type
A_{2n}
on which

\sigma
acts.
\underline{I}_{0}^{\prime}=\{\underline{1},\underline{2},\dots,\underline{n}\}
, and
\underline{\varDelta}_{0}^{\prime}

is of type
C_{n}
with
\alpha_{\underline{n}}
long root.
Take
\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}
and write it as
\alpha=\sum_{i\in I_{0}}c_{i}\alpha_{i}
.
Here
\theta=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{2n+1}
, and
\sigma(\alpha_{2n+1})=\delta-\theta
.
Assume that
\alpha\in\varSigma_{0}^{+}
.
We have

\displaystyle\alpha+\sigma(\alpha)=c_{2n+1}(\delta-\theta+\alpha_{2n+1})+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}(c_{i}+c_{2n+1-i})(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{2n+1-i})

Hence
c_{2n+1}=1
and the condition
\alpha\in\varSigma_{0}^{+}
is given by

c_{i}+c_{2n+1-i}=1,\quad(1\leq i\leq n).

This implies that
\alpha=\alpha_{n+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{2n+1}=\varepsilon_{n+1}-\varepsilon_{2n+2}
.
If we write
\varDelta_{0}^{+}=\{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq 2n+2\}
,
we have

(5.6.1)

\varSigma_{0}^{+}=\{\varepsilon_{n+1}-\varepsilon_{2n+2}\}.

Hence
\varSigma_{0}^{+}
is a positive subsystem of
\varDelta_{0}^{+}
of type
A_{1}
.

5.7.

We shall describe the set of positive real roots for
\varDelta
appearing
as
\underline{\varDelta}
under the isomorphism
(Q^{\sigma})^{\prime}\simeq\underline{Q}
in 5.1.
Let
\varDelta
be the irreducible root system
of type
X_{n}^{(r)}
with vertex set
I=\{0,1,\dots,n\}
, where

I_{0}=\{1,\dots,n\}
corresponds to the finite system
I_{0}
. In the case
where
X_{n}^{(r)}\neq A_{2n}^{(2)}
, the choice of 0 is determined uniquely,
up to the diagram automorphisms, in which case, we follow the ordering of [K].
While in the case where
X_{n}^{(r)}=A_{2n}^{(2)}
, we use the ordering
as in (
A_{a}1
) in 1.5, which is the ordering used in [BN], and is opposite to the
one in [K]. We denote by
\varDelta_{0,l}^{+}
the set of positive long roots in
\varDelta_{0}
,
and
\varDelta_{0,s}^{+}
the set of positive short roots in
\varDelta_{0}
.
Let
\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}
be the set of positive real roots in
\varDelta
.

Assume that
X_{n}^{(r)}\neq A_{2n}^{(2)}
. In this case,
\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}
is
written as
\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}=\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}_{>}\sqcup\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}_{<}
, where

(5.7.1)

\displaystyle\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}_{>}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha+m\delta\mid\alpha\in\varDelta_{0,s}^{+},m\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}\}\sqcup\{\alpha+mr\delta\mid\alpha\in\varDelta_{0,l}^{+},m\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}\},

\displaystyle\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}_{<}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha+m\delta\mid\alpha\in-\varDelta_{0,s}^{+},m\in\mathbf{Z}_{>0}\}\sqcup\{\alpha+mr\delta\mid\alpha\in-\varDelta_{0,l}^{+},m\in\mathbf{Z}_{>0}\}.

Next assume that
X_{n}^{(r)}=A_{2n}^{(2)}
. In this case,
\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}

is given as follows. Note that since our ordering is opposite to [K],
this description of
\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}
is different from the one in [K].
We fix the inner product
(\ ,\ )
so that
(\alpha,\alpha)=1,2,4
.
We give the ordering of
A_{2n}^{(2)}
as in 1.5, namely

\alpha_{0}
is such that
(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{0})=4
.
The finite root system
\varDelta_{0}
corresponding to
I_{0}
is of
type
B_{n}
.
Then
\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}
is given as

\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}=\varDelta^{+}_{s}\sqcup\varDelta^{+}_{l}\sqcup\varDelta^{+}_{2s}
,
where

(5.7.2)

\displaystyle\varDelta^{+}_{s}

\displaystyle=\{m\delta+\alpha\mid\alpha\in\varDelta^{+}_{0,s},m\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}\}\sqcup\{m\delta-\alpha\mid\alpha\in\varDelta^{+}_{0,s},m\in\mathbf{Z}_{>0}\},

\displaystyle\varDelta^{+}_{l}

\displaystyle=\{m\delta+\alpha\mid\alpha\in\varDelta^{+}_{0,l},m\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}\}\sqcup\{m\delta-\alpha\mid\alpha\in\varDelta^{+}_{0,l},m\in\mathbf{Z}_{>0}\},

\displaystyle\varDelta^{+}_{2s}

\displaystyle=\{m\delta+2\alpha\mid\alpha\in\varDelta_{0,s},m\text{ : odd integer $\geq 0$, }\},

where
\varDelta_{0,s}
is the set of short roots in
\varDelta_{0}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\varDelta^{+}_{s}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\varDelta^{+}\mid(\alpha,\alpha)=1\},

\displaystyle\varDelta^{+}_{l}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\varDelta^{+}\mid(\alpha,\alpha)=2\},

\displaystyle\varDelta^{+}_{2s}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\varDelta^{+}\mid(\alpha,\alpha)=4\}.

5.8.

Let
\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}
(resp.
\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}
) be the set of long roots
(resp. short roots) in
\underline{\varDelta}_{0}
. We denote by

\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}^{+},\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}^{+}
the corresponding positive roots.
Set

(5.8.1)

\displaystyle\varSigma_{s}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}^{+}\},

\displaystyle\varSigma_{l}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}^{+}\},

\displaystyle\varSigma_{l^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)\in\delta+\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}\},

\displaystyle\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)\in 2\delta+\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}\},

\displaystyle\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)\in 3\delta+\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}\},

\displaystyle\varSigma_{s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)\in\delta+\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}\},

\displaystyle\varSigma_{2s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)\in\delta+2\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}\},

\displaystyle\varSigma_{2s^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)\in 3\delta+2\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}\},

\displaystyle\varSigma_{0}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)\in\mathbf{Z}_{>0}\delta\}.

\varSigma_{0}^{+}
is already defined, and was determined in 5.3
\sim
5.6.
Note that
\varSigma_{2s^{\prime}},\varSigma_{2s^{\prime\prime}}
occurs only in the case where

(X,\underline{X})=(D_{2n}^{(1)},A_{2n-2}^{(2)})
.
We define
\varSigma_{l^{\prime}}^{+},\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime}}^{+},\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime\prime}}^{+}

(resp.
\varSigma_{s^{\prime}}^{+},\varSigma_{2s^{\prime}}^{+},\varSigma_{2s^{\prime\prime}}^{+}
)
by replacing

\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}
by
\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}^{+}
(resp. by replacing
\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}

by
\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}^{+}
) in the corresponding formulas.

5.9.

Let
\theta
be the highest root in
\varDelta^{+}_{0}
. Then we have

\alpha_{0}=\delta-\theta
.
Let
\underline{\theta}
be the highest root in
\underline{\varDelta}_{0}^{+}
, and

\underline{\theta}_{s}
the highest short root in
\underline{\varDelta}_{0}^{+}
. The following relations
are verified by a direct computation.

(5.9.1)

O(\alpha_{0})=\begin{cases}\delta-2\underline{\theta}_{s}&\quad\text{ Case (A), }\\
\delta-\underline{\theta}&\quad\text{ Case (B), }\\
\delta-\underline{\theta}_{s}&\quad\text{ Case (C), }\\
2\delta-\underline{\theta}&\quad\text{ Case (D). }\end{cases}

This implies that

(5.9.2)

O(\theta)=\begin{cases}3\delta+2\underline{\theta}_{s}&\quad\text{ Case (A), }\\
3\delta+\underline{\theta}&\quad\text{ Case (B), }\\
\delta+\underline{\theta}_{s}&\quad\text{ Case (C), }\\
2\delta+\underline{\theta}&\quad\text{ Case (D). }\end{cases}

Note that
O(\theta)=2(\theta+\sigma(\theta))
in Case (D).

Let
W
be the Wyl group of
\varDelta
, and
\underline{W}
the Weyl group of
\underline{\varDelta}
.
As in 3.2, the correspondence
s_{\eta}\mapsto t_{\eta}
gives an isomorphism

\underline{W}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,W^{\sigma}
.
Let
\underline{W}_{0}
be the Weyl group associated to
\underline{\varDelta}_{0}
.
Then
\underline{W}_{0}
is regarded as a subgroup of
\underline{W}
, and
under the identification
\underline{W}\simeq W^{\sigma}
, we regard
\underline{W}_{0}
as a subgroup
of
W
.

We prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.10
.

Under the notation in (5.8.1), we have

\varDelta_{0}^{+}=\begin{cases}\varSigma_{s}^{+}\sqcup\varSigma_{l}^{+}\sqcup\varSigma_{l^{\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{2s^{\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{2s^{\prime\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{0}^{+}&\quad\text{Case {\rm(A)}, }\\
\varSigma_{s}^{+}\sqcup\varSigma_{l}^{+}\sqcup\varSigma_{s^{\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{l^{\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{0}^{+}&\quad\text{Case {\rm(B)}, }\\
\varSigma_{s}^{+}\sqcup\varSigma_{l}^{+}\sqcup\varSigma_{s^{\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{0}^{+}&\quad\text{Case {\rm(C)}, }\\
\varSigma_{s}^{+}\sqcup\varSigma_{l}^{+}\sqcup\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{0}^{+}&\quad\text{Case {\rm(D)}.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Set

Y=\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0}^{+}\}=\varSigma_{s}^{+}\sqcup\varSigma_{l}^{+}.

We prove the lemma in a similar way as in [SZ2, Lemma 5.5], separately
for each case.

Case (A). Let
E
be the union of
\underline{W}_{0}
-orbits of

\sigma(\alpha_{0})
and of
\theta
, and set
E^{+}=E\cap\varDelta_{0}^{+}
.
We know by (5.9.1) and (5.9.2) that
O(\alpha_{0})=\delta-2\underline{\theta}_{s}
and

O(\theta)=3\delta+2\underline{\theta}_{s}
. Since any short root in
\underline{\varDelta}_{0}
is
\underline{W}_{0}
-conjugate
to
\underline{\theta}_{s}
, we see that for any
\alpha\in E^{+}
,
O(\alpha)
can be written as

\delta+2\underline{\beta}
or
3\delta+2\underline{\beta}
, where
\underline{\beta}\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}
.
Hence
\alpha\in\varSigma_{2s^{\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{2s^{\prime\prime}}
.
It follows that

E^{+}\subset\varSigma_{2s^{\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{2s^{\prime\prime}}\subset\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{l^{\prime}}-\varSigma_{0}^{+}.

We show that these inclusion relations are all equalities.
For any
\underline{\beta}\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}
, there exists at least three

\alpha\in E^{+}
such that
O(\alpha)=\delta+2\underline{\beta}
, and at least one
\alpha\in E^{+}

such that
O(\alpha)=3\delta+2\underline{\beta}
. It follows that

4|\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}|\leq|E^{+}|
.
On the other hand, under the notation of 5.4, we see that

\varDelta_{0}^{+}=\{\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq 2n\}
, and

\varSigma_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{l^{\prime}}=\{\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{j}\mid 2\leq j\leq 2n\}\cup\{\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{2n}\mid 2\leq i\leq 2n-1\}.

By (5.4.7),
(\varSigma_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{l^{\prime}})\cap\varSigma_{0}^{+}=\{\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2n}\}
.
Hence
|\varSigma_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{l^{\prime}}-\varSigma_{0}^{+}|=8n-8
. As
\underline{\varDelta}_{0}
is of type

B_{n-1}
, we have
|\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}|=2(n-1)
, hence

|E^{+}|=|\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{l^{\prime}}-\varSigma_{0}^{+}|
. It follows that

(5.10.1)

E^{+}=\varSigma_{2s^{\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{2s^{\prime\prime}}=\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{l^{\prime}}-\varSigma_{0}^{+}.

The required formula follows from this.

Case (B) First note that, under the notation in 5.4, for
\alpha=\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\in Z_{2}
,
we have
\alpha+\sigma(\alpha)=\delta+(\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}-\varepsilon_{2n+2-i}-\varepsilon_{2n+2-j})
.
This comes from (5.4.3).
The condition that
\alpha+\sigma(\alpha)\in\varDelta^{\operatorname{re}}
is given by
2n+2-i=i
, namely,

i=n+1
. Hence we have

(5.10.2)

\{\alpha\in Z_{2}\mid O(\alpha)\in 2\delta+\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}\}=\{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{n+1}\mid 2\leq i\leq 2n,i\neq n+1\}.

Let
E
be the union of
\underline{W}_{0}
-orbits of
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}\in\varDelta_{0}
,
where
O(\beta_{k})\in k\delta+\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}
for
k=1,2,3
, and set
E^{+}=E\cap\varDelta_{0}^{+}
.
By (5.9.1) and (5.9.2), we have
O(\alpha_{0})=\delta-\underline{\theta},O(\theta)=3\delta+\underline{\theta}
,
we may choose
\beta_{1}=\sigma(\alpha_{0}),\beta_{3}=\theta
.
It follows from (5.10.2), we may choose, for example,
\beta_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{n+1}
.
Then we have

E^{+}\subset\varSigma_{l^{\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime\prime}}\subset\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{s^{\prime}}-\varSigma_{0}^{+}.

We show that these inclusion relations are actually equalities.
For each
\underline{\beta}\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}
, there exist, at least three
\alpha\in E^{+}

such that
O(\alpha)=\delta+\underline{\beta}
, at least one
\alpha\in E^{+}
such that

O(\alpha)=2\delta+\underline{\beta}
, and at least one
\alpha\in E^{+}
such that
O(\alpha)=3\delta+\underline{\beta}

(note that
\beta_{2}\in Z_{2}
). It follows that
5|\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}|\leq|E^{+}|
.
On the other hand, by the computation for
Z_{2},Z_{4}
in 5.4, and by (5.10.2),

\displaystyle\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{j}\mid 2\leq j\leq 2n+1\}

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup\{\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{2n+1}\mid 2\leq i\leq 2n\}

\displaystyle\quad\sqcup\{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{n+1}\mid 2\leq i\leq 2n,i\neq n+1\}.

The first two sets are contained in
Z_{4}
and the third one is contained in
Z_{2}

(see (5.4.5) and (5.10.2)).
By (5.4.11), we have

(\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{s^{\prime}})\cap\varSigma_{0}^{+}=\{\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2n+1},\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{n+1},\varepsilon_{n+1}\pm\varepsilon_{2n+1}\}.

It follows that

|\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{s^{\prime}}-\varSigma_{0}^{+}|=10(n-1).

Since
\underline{\varDelta}_{0}
is of type
C_{n-1}
, we have
|\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}|=2(n-1)
,
and so
|\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{s^{\prime}}-\varSigma_{0}^{+}|=|E^{+}|
. Hence

(5.10.3)

E^{+}=\varSigma_{l^{\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime\prime}}=\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{s^{\prime}}-\varSigma_{0}^{+}.

The required formula follows from this.

Case (C) In this case,
\varDelta_{0}^{+}=\{\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq 2n+1\}
.
The action of
\sigma
on
\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}
is given as in (5.4.3).
It follows that

(5.10.4)

\{\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}\mid O(\alpha)\in 2\delta+\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}\}=\{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{n+1}\mid 1\leq i\leq 2n+1,i\neq n+1\}.

Let
E
be the union of
\underline{W}_{0}
-orbit of
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}

such that
O(\beta_{1})\in\delta+\underline{\varSigma}_{0,s}
,
\beta_{2}\in 2\delta+\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}
,
and set
E^{+}=E\cap\varDelta^{+}
.
By (5.9.2), we may choose
\beta_{1}=\theta
. By (5.10.4), we may choose, for example,

\beta_{2}=\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{n+1}
.
Then we have

E^{+}\subset\varSigma_{s^{\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime}}\subset\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{0}^{+}.

We show that these inclusion relations are actually equalities.
For each
\underline{\beta}\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}
, there exists at least one
\alpha\in E^{+}

such that
O(\alpha)\in\delta+\underline{\beta}
, and for each
\underline{\beta}^{\prime}\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}
,
there exists at least one
\alpha\in E^{+}
such that
O(\alpha)\in 2\delta+\underline{\beta}^{\prime}
.
It follows that
|E^{+}|\geq|\underline{\varDelta}_{0}|
.
On the other hand, we have

\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y=\{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq 2n+1\}.

By (5.5.2),
\varSigma_{0}^{+}\subset\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y
, and
|\varSigma_{0}^{+}|=n
.
It follows that
|\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{0}^{+}|=2n^{2}
.
Since
\underline{\varDelta}_{0}
is of type
C_{n}
,
|\underline{\varDelta}_{0}|=2n^{2}
.
Thus we have

(5.10.5)

E^{+}=\varSigma_{s^{\prime}}\sqcup\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime}}=\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{0}^{+}.

The required formula follows from this.

Case (D) Let
E
be the
\underline{W}_{0}
-orbit of
\theta
, and set

E^{+}=E\cap\varDelta_{0}^{+}
. By (5.9.2),
O(\theta)=2\delta+\underline{\theta}
.
Then we have

E^{+}\subset\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime}}\subset\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{0}^{+}.

We show that these inclusions are actually equalities.
We have
|E^{+}|\geq|\varDelta_{0,l}|
. On the other hand,

\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y=\{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{2n+2}\}.

Since
\varSigma_{0}^{+}=\{\varepsilon_{n+1}-\varepsilon_{2n+2}\}
, we see that

|\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{0}^{+}|=2n
.
Since
\underline{\varDelta}_{0}
is of type
C_{n}
, we have
|\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}|=2n
.
Thus we have

E^{+}=\varSigma_{l^{\prime\prime}}=\varDelta_{0}^{+}-Y-\varSigma_{0}^{+}.

The required formula follows from this.

The lemma is proved.
∎

5.11.

For each
m\geq 0
,
x\in\{s,2s^{\prime},2s^{\prime\prime},l,l^{\prime},l^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime\prime}\}
,
we define subsets
\varSigma_{x}(m),\varSigma_{x}^{\prime}(m)
of
\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}
as

(5.11.1)

\displaystyle\varSigma_{x}(m)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i}\sigma^{i}\{m\delta+\alpha\mid\alpha\in\varSigma_{x}^{+}\},

\displaystyle\varSigma_{x}^{\prime}(m)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i}\sigma^{i}\{m\delta-\alpha\mid\alpha\in\varSigma_{x}^{+}\},

where we assume that
m\geq 1
for
\varSigma_{x}^{\prime}(m)
.
We define an equivalence relation on
\varDelta^{\operatorname{re}}

by the condition that
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in\varDelta^{\operatorname{re}}
are equivalent
if
O(\beta)=O(\beta^{\prime})
.
We denote by
\widehat{\varSigma}_{x}(m)
the set of equivalence classes in
\varSigma_{x}(m)
,
and define
\widehat{\varSigma}_{x}^{\prime}(m)
similarly.
Let
\widehat{\varDelta}^{\operatorname{re},+}
be the set of equivalence classes in

\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}-\bigcup_{m\geq 0}\{m\delta\pm\alpha\mid\alpha\in\varSigma_{0}^{+}\}
.
For
x=0
and
m\geq 0
, we set

(5.11.2)

\varSigma_{0}(m)=\{m\delta+\alpha\mid\alpha\in\varSigma_{0}^{+}\},\qquad\varSigma^{\prime}_{0}(m)=\{m\delta-\alpha\mid\alpha\in\varSigma_{0}^{+}\}.

Also for each
m\geq 0
, we define subsets of
\underline{\varDelta}^{\operatorname{re},+}
by

(5.11.3)

\displaystyle\underline{\varSigma}_{s}(m)

\displaystyle=\{m\delta+\alpha\mid\alpha\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}^{+}\},

\displaystyle\quad\underline{\varSigma}_{s}^{\prime}(m)

\displaystyle=\{m\delta-\alpha\mid\alpha\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}^{+}\},

\displaystyle\underline{\varSigma}_{2s}(m)

\displaystyle=\{m\delta+2\alpha\mid\alpha\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}^{+}\},

\displaystyle\quad\underline{\varSigma}_{2s}^{\prime}(m)

\displaystyle=\{m\delta-2\alpha\mid\alpha\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,s}^{+}\},

\displaystyle\underline{\varSigma}_{l}(m)

\displaystyle=\{m\delta+\alpha\mid\alpha\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}^{+}\},

\displaystyle\quad\underline{\varSigma}_{l}^{\prime}(m)

\displaystyle=\{m\delta-\alpha\mid\alpha\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0,l}^{+}\}.

Note that we assume
m\geq 1
in the right hand sides of (5.11.2) and (5.11.3).

The following result is a generalization of [SZ2, Prop. 5.7].
The proof is straightforward from Lemma 5.10, by using the description of
the root system
\underline{\varDelta}^{\operatorname{re},+}
given in 5.7.

Proposition 5.12
.

Under the identification
(Q^{\sigma})^{\prime}\simeq\underline{Q}
, the followings hold.

(i)

For each
\beta\in\varDelta^{+}
,
O(\beta)\in\underline{\varDelta}^{+}
.

(ii)

In the cases
(A), (B), (C),

we have a partition

\widehat{\varDelta}^{\operatorname{re},+}=\bigsqcup_{x,m\geq 0}\widehat{\varSigma}_{x}(m)\sqcup\bigsqcup_{x,m\geq 1}\widehat{\varSigma}_{x}^{\prime}(m).

The map
f:\beta\mapsto O(\beta)
induces a bijection

\widehat{\varDelta}^{\operatorname{re},+}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varDelta}^{\operatorname{re},+}
.
The bijection is given more precisely as follows.
(In the formulas below, we only give the part corresponding to
\widehat{\varSigma}_{x}(m)
.
The part corresponding to
\widehat{\varSigma}_{x}^{\prime}(m)
is described similarly.).

Case
(A)

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{s}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{s}(m),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{l}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{l}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 0\mod 2),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{l^{\prime}}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{l}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 1\mod 2),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{2s^{\prime}}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{2s}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 1\mod 4),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{2s^{\prime\prime}}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{2s}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 3\mod 4).

Case
(B)

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{s}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{s}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 0\mod 2),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{s^{\prime}}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{s}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 1\mod 2),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{l}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{l}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 0\mod 4),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{l^{\prime}}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{l}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 1\mod 4),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{l^{\prime\prime}}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{l}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 2\mod 4),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{l^{\prime\prime\prime}}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{l}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 3\mod 4).

Case
(C)

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{s}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{s}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 0\mod 2),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{s^{\prime}}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{s}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 1\mod 2),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{l}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{l}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 0\mod 4),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{l^{\prime\prime}}(m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{l}(m),

\displaystyle(m\equiv 2\mod 4).

(iii)

In the case
(D)
,
we have a partition

\widehat{\varDelta}^{\operatorname{re},+}=\bigsqcup_{x,m\geq 0}\widehat{\varSigma}_{x}(2m)\sqcup\bigsqcup_{x,m\geq 0}\widehat{\varSigma}_{x}^{\prime}(2m+1).

For
\beta\in\varSigma_{x}(2m)
(resp.
\beta\in\varSigma_{x}(2m+1)
),

O(\beta)
is written as
O(\beta)=2m\delta+\underline{\beta}
(resp.
O(\beta)=(2m+1)\delta+\underline{\beta}
)
with
\underline{\beta}\in\underline{\varDelta}_{0}^{+}
. Define
f:\widehat{\varSigma}_{x}(2m)\to\underline{\varDelta}

(resp.
f:\widehat{\varSigma}_{x}(2m+1)\to\underline{\varDelta}
by
f(\beta)=m\delta+\underline{\beta}
.
Then
f
induces a bijection
\widehat{\varDelta}^{\operatorname{re},+}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varDelta}^{\operatorname{re},+}
.
More precisely, the map is given as follows;

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{s}(2m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{s}(m),

\displaystyle\widehat{\varSigma}_{l}(2m)\sqcup\widehat{\varSigma}_{l^{\prime\prime}}(2m)

\displaystyle\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\underline{\varSigma}_{l}(m).

The case for
\widehat{\varSigma}_{x}(2m+1)
is given similarly.

6.
Modified PBW-bases – affine case

6.1.

We keep the assumption in 5.1.
We consider the doubly infinite sequence

\underline{\mathbf{h}}=(\dots,\eta_{-1},\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\dots)
for

\underline{W}
associated to
\underline{\xi}\in\underline{P}^{\vee}_{\operatorname{cl}}
as defined in [SZ2, 1.5],
applied for
\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}
. We define a sequence

\mathbf{h}^{\prime}=(\dots,i_{-1},i_{0},i_{1},\dots)
by replacing
\eta=\eta_{i}
by
a sequence
J_{\eta}
in
I
, where

J_{\eta}=\begin{cases}(j_{1},\dots,j_{|\eta|})&\quad\text{ if $\delta_{\eta}=1$ and }\eta=\{j_{1},\dots,j_{|\eta|}\},\\
(i,j,i)&\quad\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.31946pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces if $\delta_{\eta}=2$ and $\eta=\{i,j\}$. }\crcr}}}}\end{cases}}

Thus

(6.1.1)

\mathbf{h}^{\prime}=(\dots,\underbrace{i_{-k-k^{\prime\prime}},\dots,i_{-k-1}}_{J_{\eta_{-1}}}\underbrace{i_{-k},\dots,i_{-1},i_{0}}_{J_{\eta_{0}}},\underbrace{i_{1},\dots,i_{k^{\prime}}}_{J_{\eta_{1}}},\cdots).

For any
k<\ell
,
\underline{w}=s_{\eta_{k}}\cdots s_{\eta\ell}
is a reduced expression
of
\underline{w}\in\underline{W}
.
We define
w\in W
by

(6.1.2)

w=t_{\eta_{k}}\cdots t_{\eta_{\ell}},

where
t_{\eta}
is defined as in 3.2.
In view of Proposition 5.12,

\varDelta^{+}\cap w^{-1}(-\varDelta^{+})=f^{-1}(\underline{\varDelta}^{+}\cap\underline{w}^{-1}(-\underline{\varDelta}^{+})).

It follows that (6.1.2) is a reduced expression of
w
, and
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
gives
an infinite reduced word.
We define
\underline{\beta}_{k}\in\underline{\varDelta}^{\operatorname{re},+}
for any
k\in\mathbf{Z}
by

(6.1.3)

\underline{\beta}_{k}=\begin{cases}s_{\eta_{0}}s_{\eta_{-1}}\cdots s_{\eta_{k+1}}(\underline{\alpha}_{\eta_{k}})&\quad\text{ if $k\leq 0$, }\\
s_{\eta_{1}}s_{\eta_{2}}\cdots s_{\eta_{k-1}}(\underline{\alpha}_{\eta_{k}})&\quad\text{ if $k>0$. }\end{cases}

Then by [BN, 3.1], we have

(6.1.4)

\underline{\varDelta}_{>}^{\operatorname{re},+}=\{\underline{\beta}_{k}\mid k\leq 0\},\qquad\underline{\varDelta}_{<}^{\operatorname{re},+}=\{\underline{\beta}_{k}\mid k>0\}.

Also define
\beta_{k}\in\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}
for
k\in\mathbf{Z}
by

(6.1.5)

\beta_{k}=\begin{cases}s_{i_{0}}s_{i_{-1}}\cdots s_{i_{k+1}}(\alpha_{i_{k}})&\quad\text{ if $k\leq 0$, }\\
s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\cdots s_{i_{k-1}}(\alpha_{i_{k}})&\quad\text{ if $k>0$. }\end{cases}

Recall
\varSigma_{x}(m),\varSigma_{x}^{\prime}(m)
in (5.11.1) for

x\in\mathscr{X}=\{s,2s^{\prime},2s^{\prime\prime},l,l^{\prime},l^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime\prime}\}

and
\varSigma_{0}(m)
,
\varSigma_{0}^{\prime}(m)
in (5.11.2).
We define
\varDelta^{(0)}_{>},\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}
by

\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}=\bigsqcup_{m\geq 0}\bigsqcup_{x\in\mathscr{X}}\varSigma_{x}(m),\qquad\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}=\bigsqcup_{m\geq 1}\bigsqcup_{x\in\mathscr{X}}\varSigma^{\prime}_{x}(m).

Also set

\varDelta^{(1)}_{>}=\bigsqcup_{m\geq 0}\varSigma_{0}(m),\qquad\varDelta^{(1)}_{<}=\bigsqcup_{m\geq 1}\varSigma^{\prime}_{0}(m).

Then
\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}
and
\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}
are
\sigma
-stable, and

\varDelta^{(1)}_{>}\sqcup\varDelta^{(1)}_{<}
is
\sigma
-stable.
By Proposition 5.12 and (6.1.4), we see that

(6.1.6)

\{\beta_{k}\mid k\leq 0\}=\varDelta^{(0)}_{>},\qquad\{\beta_{k},\mid k>0\}=\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}.

Moreover, we have

\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}=\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}\sqcup\varDelta^{(1)}_{>}\sqcup\varDelta^{(1)}_{<}\sqcup\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}
.

6.2.

Hereafter we apply the theory of PBW-basis associated to the convex order
developed by Muthiah-Tingley [MT] to our setup.
We follow the notation in [SZ2, Section 4].
For any real root
\beta=\alpha+m\delta\in\varDelta^{\operatorname{re}}
, we denote by
\overline{\beta}=\alpha\in\varDelta_{0}
.
Set
\varDelta_{+}^{\min}=\varDelta^{\operatorname{re},+}\sqcup\{\delta\}
.
For any subset
Z
of
\varDelta_{+}^{\min}
, we define a total order
\alpha\preceq\beta
,
called a convex order,
by the following two conditions,

(6.2.1) If
\alpha,\beta\in Z
with
\alpha+\beta\in Z
, then
\alpha+\beta
is in between

\alpha
and
\beta
.

(6.2.2) If
\alpha\in Z,\beta\in\varDelta_{+}^{\min}-Z
with
\alpha+\beta\in Z
, then

\alpha\prec\alpha+\beta
.

If in (6.2.2), the condition “
\alpha\prec\alpha+\beta
” is replaced by
“
\alpha+\beta\prec\alpha
”, such a total order is called a reverse convex order.

We define a total order
\prec
on
\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}
by

\beta_{0}\prec\beta_{-1}\prec\beta_{-2}\cdots
, and a total order
\prec
on
\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}

by
\cdots\prec\beta_{2}\prec\beta_{1}
. Then by [SZ2, Lemma 4.3],
\prec
gives a convex order
on
\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}
and a reverse convex order on
\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}
.
This implies that

(6.2.3)
\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}\prec\delta\prec\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}
satisfies the condition
(6.2.1). In particular,
\beta\prec\delta+\beta
for
\beta\in\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}

and
\beta+\delta\prec\beta
for
\beta\in\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}
.

We shall define a total order on
\varDelta^{(1)}_{>}
and on
\varDelta^{(1)}_{<}
.
In the case where
(X,\underline{X})
is in the case (A), (C), ot (D),
\varSigma_{0}^{+}

is a positive subsystem of
\varDelta^{+}
of type
nA_{1}
for some
n\geq 1
.
In this case, write
\varSigma_{0}^{+}=\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{t}\}
in any order. We define
a total order on
\varSigma^{(1)}_{>}
by the condition that
m\delta+\gamma_{i}\prec m^{\prime}\delta+\gamma_{j}

if
m<m^{\prime}
or if
m=m^{\prime}
and
i<j
.
Similarly, we define a total order on
\varDelta^{(1)}_{<}
by the condition that

m\delta-\gamma_{i}\prec m^{\prime}\delta-\gamma_{j}
if
m>m^{\prime}
or if
m=m^{\prime}
and
i>j
.

We now consider the case (B). In this case
\varDelta_{0}^{+}
is a positive subsystem
in
\varDelta^{+}
of type
(n-1)A_{1}+A_{3}
by (5.4.11).
Thus
\varDelta_{0}^{+}=X_{1}\sqcup X_{2}
, where
X_{1}
is a positive system of type

A_{3}
and
X_{2}
is a positive system of type
(n-1)A_{1}
.
Let
\varDelta^{(1)}
be the subsystem of
\varDelta

spanned by
\gamma\in X_{1},\gamma_{0}=\delta-\theta_{1}
, and
\gamma^{\prime},\delta-\gamma^{\prime}
for
\gamma^{\prime}\in X_{2}
,
where
\theta_{1}
is the highest root in
X_{1}
.
Then
\varDelta^{(1)}
is an affine root system of type
A_{3}^{(1)}+(n-1)A_{1}^{(1)}
.
We denote by
W_{1}
the Wyl group of
\varDelta^{(1)}
. We consider the infinite reduced
word
\mathbf{h}^{(1)}=(\dots,i_{-1},i_{0},i_{1},\dots)
of
W_{1}
defined in [BN] as
in [SZ, 1.5], and define
\beta_{k}^{\prime}\in(\varDelta^{(1)})^{\operatorname{re},+}
for
k\in\mathbf{Z}

as in (6.1.5). Then we have

(6.2.4)

\{\beta^{\prime}_{k}\mid k\leq 0\}=\varDelta^{(1)}_{>},\qquad\{\beta^{\prime}_{k}\mid k>0\}=\varDelta^{(1)}_{<}.

Note that the discussion in the case (B) works also for the cases (A), (C), (D).
Thus again by [SZ2, Lemma 4.3], we see that

(6.2.5).
\varDelta^{(1)}_{>}\prec\delta\prec\varDelta^{(1)}_{<}
satisfies the
condition (6.2.1).

We define a total order
\prec
on
\varDelta_{+}^{\min}=\varDelta^{+}\cup\{\delta\}

by extending the total order on
\varDelta^{(i)}_{>}
and
\varDelta^{(i)}_{<}
for
i=1,2
,
under the condition

(6.2.6)

\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}\prec\varDelta^{(1)}_{>}\prec\delta\prec\varDelta^{(1)}_{<}\prec\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}.

Recall that the coarse type of the convex order
\preceq
on
\varDelta_{+}^{\min}

is defined as the unique
\overline{w}\in W_{0}
such that

\overline{w}(\varDelta^{+}_{0})=\{\overline{\beta}\in\varDelta_{0}\mid\beta\prec\delta\}
(see [SZ2, 4.8]).
The convex order is called the standard type if the coarse type
\overline{w}
coincides with
the identity element in
W_{0}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

The total order
\prec
gives a convex order on
\varDelta_{+}^{\min}
.

Proof.

We show that
\prec
satisfies the condition (6.2.1) for
X=\varDelta_{+}^{\min}
.
Note that in this case, the condition (6.2.2) is redundant.
It is enough to consider the case where
\beta\in\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}\sqcup\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}

and
\beta^{\prime}\in\varDelta^{(1)}_{>}\sqcup\varDelta^{(1)}_{<}
.
Take
\beta\in\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}
, and assume that
\gamma=\beta+\beta^{\prime}\in\varDelta^{+}
.
We show that
\gamma\in\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}
and
\beta\prec\gamma
.
We have
O(\gamma)=O(\beta)+O(\beta^{\prime})\notin\mathbf{Z}_{>0}\delta
since
O(\beta)\notin\mathbf{Z}_{>0}\delta

and
O(\beta^{\prime})\in\mathbf{Z}_{>0}\delta
. Thus
\gamma\in\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}\sqcup\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}
.
If
\beta^{\prime}\in\varDelta^{(1)}_{>}
, we must have
\gamma\in\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}
, and

\beta\prec\gamma
(see (6.2.3)). So consider the case where
\beta^{\prime}\in\varDelta^{(1)}_{<}
.
Suppose that
\gamma\in\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}
. We can write as
\beta=m\delta+\alpha
,

\beta^{\prime}=m^{\prime}\delta-\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta+\beta^{\prime}=m^{\prime\prime}\delta-\alpha^{\prime\prime}
, where
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in\varDelta_{x}^{+}

and
\alpha^{\prime}\in\varDelta_{0}^{+}
. Then we have
\alpha+\alpha^{\prime\prime}=\alpha^{\prime}
.
This contradicts
\alpha^{\prime}\in\varSigma_{0}^{+}
. Hence
\gamma\in\varSigma^{(0)}_{>}
, and
we have
\beta\prec\gamma
by (6.2.3). Thus
\prec
gives a convex order on
\varDelta_{+}^{\min}
.
∎

6.4.

Let
\prec
be the convex order on
\varDelta^{\min}_{+}
defined in Lemma 6.3.
Let
\mathscr{C}
be the set of triples
\mathbf{c}=(\mathbf{c}_{+},\mathbf{c}_{0},\mathbf{c}_{-})
, where

\mathbf{c}_{+}=(c_{\beta})_{\beta\prec\delta}
,

\mathbf{c}_{-}=(c_{\gamma})_{\gamma\succ\delta}
, and
\mathbf{c}_{0}=(\rho^{(i)})_{i\in I_{0}}

is an
I_{0}
-tuple of partitions.
For each finite sequence

\beta_{1}\prec\beta_{2}\prec\cdots\prec\beta_{N}\prec\delta\prec\gamma_{M}\prec\cdots\prec\gamma_{2}\prec\gamma_{1}

in
\varDelta^{\min}_{+}
, we define

(6.4.1)

L(\mathbf{c},\prec)=f_{\beta_{1}}^{\prec,(c_{\beta_{1}})}\cdots f_{\beta_{N}}^{\prec,(c_{\beta_{N}})}S_{\mathbf{c}_{0}}^{\prec}f_{\gamma_{M}}^{\prec,(c_{\gamma_{M}})}\cdots f_{\gamma_{1}}^{\prec,(c_{\gamma_{1}})}.

as in [SZ2, 4.19]. Then by the discussion in [SZ2, Section 4],

\mathscr{X}_{\prec}=\{L(\mathbf{c},\prec)\mid\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{C}\}
gives a PBW-basis of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.
The coarse type
\overline{w}\in W_{0}
of the convex order
\prec
is
given by
\overline{w}=\prod_{i\in\eta_{0}-\{0\}}s_{i}
.
Hence by [MT, Theorem 4.13] that
S_{\mathbf{c}_{0}}^{\prec}=T_{w}S_{\mathbf{c}_{0}}
, where

S_{\mathbf{c}_{0}}
is the one defined by [BN] (see [SZ2, 1.5]), and
w\in W
is the minimal
length lift of
\overline{w}\in W_{0}
.
Also by [SZ2, Lemma 6.7], for
\beta\in\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}\sqcup\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}
,

f_{\beta}^{\prec,(c)}
coincides with
f_{\beta_{k}}^{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}}
, where
for
\beta=\beta_{k}
in the notation of (6.1.6),
we define the root vector
f_{\beta_{k}}^{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}}
(with respect to the infinite reduced word

\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
in (6.1.1)) by

f_{\beta_{k}}^{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}T_{i_{0}}T_{i_{-1}}\cdots T_{i_{k+1}}f_{i_{k}}^{(c)}&\quad\text{ if }k\leq 0,\\
T_{i_{1}}^{-1}T_{i_{2}}^{-1}\cdots T_{i_{k-1}}^{-1}f_{i_{k}}^{(c)}&\quad\text{ if }k>0.\end{cases}

Remark 6.5.

In [SZ2, Lemma 6.4], it is stated that the convex order
\prec

defined there is of standard type. But this is incorrect, and the coarse type

\overline{w}\in W_{0}
is given in a similar way as above.

6.6.

Let
\prec
be a convex order of
\varDelta^{\min}_{+}
such that
\beta_{0}
is minimal,
i.e.,
\beta_{0}\prec\beta_{-1}\prec\beta_{-2}\cdots
. We assume further that
\beta_{0}=\alpha_{i}

is a simple root. Let
s_{i}=s_{\alpha_{i}}\in W
be the simple reflection.
We can define a new convex order
\prec^{s_{i}}
by moving
\beta_{0}
to the right end,
and by acting
s_{i}
on the remaining part, namely,

s_{i}(\beta_{-1})\prec^{s_{i}}s_{i}(\beta_{-2})\prec^{s_{i}}\cdots\prec^{s_{i}}s_{i}(\beta_{1})\prec^{s_{i}}\beta_{0},

where
\beta_{0}
is maximal in
\prec^{s_{i}}
.
Similarly, if
\beta_{1}=\alpha_{i}
is maximal in
\varDelta_{+}^{\min}
, i.e.,

\cdots\prec\beta_{2}\prec\beta_{1}
, we can define a new convex order
\prec^{s_{i}}

in a similar way, by moving
\beta_{1}
to the left end.

We apply this construction to our convex order on
\varDelta^{\min}_{+}
.
In this case, the part in
\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}\sqcup\varDelta_{<}^{(0)}

is determined by
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
, namely
for
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}=(\dots,i_{-2},i_{-1},i_{0},i_{1},i_{2},\dots)
, the order is given by

\beta_{0}\prec\beta_{-1}\prec\beta_{-2}\prec\cdots\prec\beta_{2}\prec\beta_{1},

where
\beta_{0}=\alpha_{i_{0}}
,
\beta_{1}=\alpha_{i_{1}}
,
and
\beta_{k}
are given as in (6.1.5).
Thus the above construction of
\prec^{s_{i}}
can be applied successively for

s_{i_{0}},s_{i_{-1}},\dots,s_{i_{k+1}}
, and one can define a convex order

\prec^{w}
for
w=s_{i_{0}}s_{i_{-1}}\cdots s_{i_{k+1}}
by

\prec^{w}=(\cdots((\prec^{s_{i_{0}}})^{s_{i_{-1}}})\cdots)^{s_{i_{k+1}}}
.
We obtain

(6.6.1)

\alpha_{i_{k}}\prec^{w}s_{i_{k}}(\alpha_{i_{k-1}})\prec^{w}s_{i_{k}}s_{i_{k-1}}(\alpha_{i_{k-2}})\prec^{w}\cdots

Similarly, one can define a convex order
\prec^{w}
for

w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k-1}}
(
k\geq 1
), and we obtain

(6.6.2)

\cdots\prec^{w}s_{i_{k}}s_{i_{k+1}}(\alpha_{i_{k+2}})\prec^{w}s_{i_{k}}(\alpha_{i_{k+1}})\prec^{w}\alpha_{i_{k}}

Here for each
p\leq 0
, we consider the subsequence

(\eta_{p},\dots,\eta_{-1},\eta_{0})
of
\underline{\mathbf{h}}
, and let

(i_{k+1},\dots,i_{-1},i_{0})
(
k\leq 0
)
be the subsequence of
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
corresponding to

(J_{\eta_{p}},\dots,J_{\eta_{0}})
.
Similarly, we define a subsequence
(i_{1},\dots,i_{k-1})
of
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}

associated to the subsequence
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\dots,\eta_{p})
for
p\geq 1
.
Let
w=s_{i_{0}}\cdots s_{i_{k+1}}
or
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k-1}}
, and
consider the convex order
\prec^{w}
defined above, which we denote by
\prec_{p}
.
Thus
\prec_{p}
gives a two-row order

\varDelta^{(0)}_{>,p}\prec_{p}\varDelta^{(1)}_{>,p}\prec_{p}\delta\prec_{p}\varDelta^{(1)}_{<,p}\prec_{p}\varDelta^{(0)}_{<,p}.

Now we define
L(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
by replacing
\prec
by
\prec_{p}
in (6.4.1).
We note, by [MT, Cor. 4.14], that

(6.6.3)

S_{\mathbf{c}_{0}}^{\prec_{p}}=\begin{cases}T_{i_{p+1}}^{-1}\cdots T_{i_{0}}^{-1}(S_{\mathbf{c}_{0}}^{\prec}),&\quad\text{ if $p\leq 0$,}\\
T_{i_{p-1}}\cdots T_{i_{2}}T_{i_{1}}(S_{\mathbf{c}_{0}}^{\prec}),&\quad\text{ if $p\geq 1$.}\end{cases}

Set

\mathscr{X}_{\prec_{p}}=\{L(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})\mid\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{C}_{p}\},

where
\mathscr{C}_{p}
is the parameter set for
\prec_{p}
.
Then

\mathscr{X}_{\prec_{p}}
gives a PBW-basis of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.

By [MT, Prop.4.18], the following holds.

(6.6.4)

\displaystyle T_{i_{p}}^{-1}L(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})

\displaystyle=L(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p-1}),

\displaystyle\text{if $p\leq 0$ and $c_{\alpha}=0$, }

\displaystyle T_{i_{p+1}}L(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})

\displaystyle=L(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p+1}),

\displaystyle\text{if $p\geq 1$ and $c_{\alpha}=0$.}

Here
\alpha=\alpha_{i_{p}}
or
\alpha=\alpha_{i_{p+1}}
according to
p\leq 0
or
p\geq 1
.

\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
is defined by
c^{\prime}_{\beta}=c_{s_{i_{p}}(\beta)}
for
\beta\neq\alpha
,

c^{\prime}_{\alpha}=0
, and the imaginary part is defined by the action of
T_{i_{p}}^{-1}

or
T_{i_{p+1}}
accordingly (see (6.6.3)).

6.7.

Following the discussion in 3.4, we modify the root vectors corresponding
to
\varDelta^{(0)}_{>,p}
and
\varDelta^{(0)}_{<,p}
. Note that for
\beta\in\varDelta^{(0)}_{>,p}

or
\beta\in\varDelta^{(0)}_{<,p}
,
f_{\beta}^{\prec}
coincides with
f_{\beta}^{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}(w)}
,
where
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}(w)
is the doubly infinite sequence obtained from
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
by the action of

w\in W
as in 6.6.
In the case where
\delta_{\eta_{k}}=1
, set

\mathbf{d}_{k}=(d_{j})_{j\in\eta_{k}}\in\mathbf{N}^{\eta_{k}}=\mathbf{N}^{J_{k}}
, where
J_{k}
is
as in 6.1. We define

(6.7.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{d}_{k})}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{p}}\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{p-1}}\cdots\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{k+1}}(\prod_{j\in\eta_{k}}f_{j}^{(d_{j})})&\quad\text{ if $p\leq 0$, }\\
\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{p+1}}^{-1}\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{p+2}}^{-1}\cdots\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{k-1}}^{-1}(\prod_{j\in\eta_{k}}f_{j}^{(d_{j})})&\quad\text{ if $p>0$. }\end{cases}

While in the case where
\delta_{\eta_{k}}=2
with
\eta_{k}=\{i,j\}
,
we consider the subalgebra
\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta_{k}}
of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
isomorphic to

\mathbf{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}_{3})^{-}
, and consider the canonical basis

\mathbf{B}_{\eta_{k}}=\{b(\mathbf{d}_{k},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}})\}
as defined in 3.4,
where
\mathbf{d}_{k}\in\mathbf{N}^{3}=\mathbf{N}^{J_{k}}
.
We define

(6.7.2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{\eta_{k}}^{(\mathbf{d}_{k})}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{p}}\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{p-1}}\cdots\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{k+1}}(b(\mathbf{d}_{k},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}}))&\quad\text{ if $p\leq 0$, }\\
\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{p+1}}^{-1}\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{p+2}}^{-1}\cdots\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{k-1}}^{-1}(b(\mathbf{d}_{k},\mathbf{h}_{\eta_{k}}))&\quad\text{ if $p>0$. }\end{cases}

In the definition of
L(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
, we replace the root vectors
belonging to
\varDelta^{(0)}_{>}
and
\varDelta^{(0)}_{<}
by (6.7.1) and (6.7.2),
and leave the root vectors belonging to
\varDelta^{(1)}_{>}\sqcup\varDelta_{<}^{(1)}

unchanged.
Thus one can define
L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d},\prec_{p})
as in 3.4.
Set

(6.7.3)

\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}=\{L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d},\prec_{p})\mid\mathbf{d}\in\mathscr{C}^{\natural}_{p}\},

where
\mathscr{C}^{\natural}_{p}=\{\mathbf{d}=(\mathbf{d}^{\natural}_{+},\mathbf{d}_{0},\mathbf{d}^{\natural}_{-})\}

is a parameter set for
\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}
. Here if we define
\mathbf{d}_{+}^{(0)}

(resp.
\mathbf{d}_{-}^{(0)}
)
as the part in
\mathbf{d}^{\natural}_{+}
(resp.
\mathbf{d}^{\natural}_{-}
) corresponding to

\varDelta^{(0)}_{>,p}
(resp.
\varDelta^{(0)}_{<,p}
),
we have

\mathbf{d}_{+}^{(0)}=(\mathbf{d}_{k})_{k\leq p}\text{ with }\mathbf{d}_{k}\in\mathbf{N}^{J_{k}},\qquad\mathbf{d}_{-}^{(0)}=(\mathbf{d}_{k})_{k>p}\text{ with }\mathbf{d}_{k}\in\mathbf{N}^{J_{k}},

where
J_{k}=J_{\eta_{k}}
.
Note that the parameter set
\mathscr{C}^{\natural}_{p}
is naturally identified with
\mathscr{C}_{p}

under the correspondence
\mathbf{d}_{k}\leftrightarrow(\cdots,c_{\beta_{j}},\cdots)_{j\in J_{k}}

(see 2.1 for the case where
\delta_{\eta_{k}}=2
).

\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}
gives an
\mathbf{A}
-basis of
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
, which we call
the modified PBW-basis of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.
As in (6.6.4), we have the following.

(6.7.4)

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{p}}^{-1}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})

\displaystyle=L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p-1}),

\displaystyle\text{ if $p\leq 0$ and $\mathbf{c}_{p}=0$, }

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{\eta_{p+1}}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})

\displaystyle=L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p+1}),

\displaystyle\text{ if $p>0$ and $\mathbf{c}_{p}=0$, }

6.8.

Let
\underline{\beta}_{0}\prec\underline{\beta}{-1}\prec\cdots\prec\delta\prec\cdots\prec\underline{\beta}_{1}

be the convex order associated to
\underline{\mathbf{h}}
. We know that

\underline{\varDelta}^{\operatorname{re},+}_{>}=\{\underline{\beta}_{k}\mid k\leq 0\}
and

\underline{\varDelta}^{\operatorname{re},+}_{<}=\{\underline{\beta}_{k}\mid k>0\}
. Thus for each
\eta\in\underline{I}
,
there exists
p\in\mathbf{Z}
such that
\eta=\eta_{p}
.
We consider the modified PBW-basis
\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}
of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.
In the case where
p\leq 0
, we denote by
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]
the
\mathbf{Q}(q)
-subspace
of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
spanned by
L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
such that
\mathbf{c}_{p}=0
, and set

{}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}=*(\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta])
, where
*:\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\to\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
is
the anti-algebra automorphism. While in the case where
p>0
, we define

{}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]
as the
\mathbf{Q}(q)
-subspace of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
spanned by

L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
such that
\mathbf{c}_{p}=0
, and set
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]=*({}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta])
.
Note that in the case where
\delta_{\eta}=1
with
\eta=\{\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,\alpha_{i_{k}}\}
,
where
k=|\eta|
, we consider
\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}
the subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
generated by

f_{i_{1}},\dots,f_{i_{k}}
. Then
\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}
is of type
A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{1}

(
k
-times), and the set

\{f_{i_{1}}^{(c_{1})}\cdots f_{i_{k}}^{(c_{k})}\mid\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{k})\in\mathbf{N}^{k}\}

coincides with the canonical basis

\mathbf{B}_{\eta}=\{b(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h}_{\eta})\mid\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{N}^{|\eta|}\}
.
In the following discussion, we write
b(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{h}_{\eta})
simply as
b(\mathbf{c}_{\eta})
,
where

\mathbf{c}_{\eta}=\begin{cases}(c_{1},\dots,c_{|\eta|})\in\mathbf{N}^{|\eta|}&\quad\text{ if $\delta_{\eta}=1$},\\
(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})\in\mathbf{N}^{3}&\quad\text{ if $\delta_{\eta}=2$}.\end{cases}

Thus even for the case (6.7.1), we use the same notation as in (6.7.2),
by using the canonical basis
\mathbf{B}_{\eta}
.

In the case where
\eta=\eta_{p}
with
p\leq 0
,
let
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}[\eta]
be the
\mathbf{A}
-submodule of
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}

spanned by
L^{\sharp}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
such that
\mathbf{c}_{p}=0
, and
set
{}_{\mathbf{A}}{}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]=*({}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta])
.
In the case where
\eta=\eta_{p}
with
p>0
, let
{}_{\mathbf{A}}{}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]

be the
\mathbf{A}
-submodule of
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
spanned by
L^{\sharp}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})

such that
\mathbf{c}_{p}=0
,
and set
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]=*({}_{\mathbf{A}}{}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta])
.

Lemma 6.9
.

(i)

We have a direct sum decomposition

(6.9.1)

\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}=\bigoplus_{b\in\mathbf{B}_{\eta}}b\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta],\qquad\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}=\bigoplus_{b\in\mathbf{B}_{\eta}}{}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]b.

(ii)

We have a direct sum decomposition of
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
as
\mathbf{A}
-modules,

(6.9.2)

{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}=\bigoplus_{b\in\mathbf{B}_{\eta}}b{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta],\qquad{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}=\bigoplus_{b\in\mathbf{B}_{\eta}}{}_{\mathbf{A}}{}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]b.

Proof.

Assume that
\eta=\eta_{p}
for
p\leq 0
. Then by using
the PBW-basis
\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}
, we obtain the first identity in (6.9.1).
Note that
\sigma
acts on
\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}
, and
\sigma
gives a permutation
on the canonical basis
\mathbf{B}_{\eta}
of
\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}
, which is the same as
the
*
-operation on
\mathbf{B}_{\eta}
. Thus the second
identity follows from the first by applying
*
on both sides.
In the case where
\eta=\eta_{p}
with
p>0
, by using
\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}
,
we obtain the second identity in (6.9.1). By applying
*
, the first identity follows.
(i) is proved. (ii) follows from (i) since
\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}
is an
\mathbf{A}
-basis of

{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.
∎

Proposition 6.10
.

Under the notation above, we have

(6.10.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\mid\widetilde{T}_{\eta}^{-1}(x)\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\},

(6.10.2)

\displaystyle{}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\mid\widetilde{T}_{\eta}(x)\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\}.

Proof.

We consider the case where
\eta=\eta_{p}
with
p\leq 0
.
By (6.7.4), we have

(6.10.3)

\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]\subset\{x\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\mid\widetilde{T}_{\eta}^{-1}(x)\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\}.

We show the converse inclusion.
Lemma 6.9 implies that the map
u\otimes v\mapsto uv
gives an isomorphism
as vector spaces
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}\simeq\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]
.
Hence the proof of (6.10.1) is reduced to the case where
\mathbf{U}^{-}_{q}=\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}
.
It is enough to see that

\{x\in\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}\mid\widetilde{T}_{\eta}^{-1}(x)\in\mathbf{L}^{-}_{\eta}\}=\{0\}
.
In general, we consider the braid group action
T_{w}:\mathbf{U}_{q}\to\mathbf{U}_{q}
.
We have a weight space decomposition
\mathbf{U}_{q}=\bigoplus_{\nu\in Q}(\mathbf{U}_{q})_{\nu}
, and
we know that
T_{w}:(\mathbf{U}_{q})_{\nu}\to(\mathbf{U}_{q})_{w(\nu)}
.
Hence

\mathbf{L}_{\eta}^{-}\cap\widetilde{T}_{\eta}(\mathbf{L}_{\eta}^{-})=\{0\}

since
\widetilde{T}_{\eta}(\mathbf{L}_{\eta}^{-})=\mathbf{L}_{\eta}^{+}
, where
\mathbf{L}_{\eta}^{+}=\mathbf{U}_{q}^{+}

is the positive part of
\mathbf{U}_{q}
.
This proves (6.10.1). Then (6.10.2) is obtained from (6.10.1) by applying
the *-operation, since
*\circ\widetilde{T}_{\eta}\,\circ*=\widetilde{T}^{-1}_{\eta}
.
In the case where
\eta=\eta_{p}
with
p>0
, (6.10.2) is proved in a similar way.
Then (6.10.1) follows from (6.10.2). The proposition is proved.
∎

6.11.

Proposition 6.10 shows that
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]
does not depend on the
choice of
\underline{\mathbf{h}}
. Note that
\widetilde{T}_{\eta}=\prod_{i\in\eta}T_{i}

for
\eta\in\underline{I}
with
\delta_{\eta}=1
, and

\widetilde{T}_{\eta}=T_{i}T_{j}T_{i}=T_{j}T_{i}T_{j}
if
\eta=\{i,j\}
with
\delta_{\eta}=2
.
Hence
\sigma\circ\widetilde{T}_{\eta}=\widetilde{T}_{\eta}\circ\sigma
. Then Proposition 6.10
implies that
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]
is
\sigma
-stable. Note that this is not directly obtained
from the definition since we don’t know that
\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}
is stable under
the action of
\sigma
.

In the case where
\delta_{\eta}=1
, the set
\mathbf{B}_{\eta}^{\sigma}
is given as

\mathbf{B}_{\eta}^{\sigma}=\{\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}=\prod_{i\in\eta}f_{i}^{(a)}\mid a\in\mathbf{N}\}
.
While in the case where
\delta_{\eta}=2
with
\eta=\{i,j\}
,

\mathbf{B}_{\eta}^{\sigma}=\{\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}=f_{i}^{(a)}f_{j}^{(2a)}f_{i}^{(a)}=f_{j}^{(a)}f_{i}^{(2a)}f_{j}^{(a)}\mid a\in\mathbf{N}\}.

The following result is obtained immediately from Lemma 6.9.
Note that the uniqueness property follows from the property of
PBW-basis
\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}
.

Proposition 6.12
.

Concerning the first formula in (6.9.1), the following holds.

(i)

We have a direct sum decomposition

\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathbf{N}}\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}(\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta])^{\sigma}\oplus K_{\eta},

where
K_{\eta}
is a sum of
bx+\sigma(bx)
for
b\in\mathbf{B}_{\eta}
such that
\sigma(b)\neq b
,
and
x\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]
.

x\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}
can be written uniquely as

x=\sum_{a\in\mathbf{N}}\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}x_{a}+y

with
x_{a}\in(\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta])^{\sigma}
and
y\in K_{\eta}
.

(ii)

We also have a decomposition as
\mathbf{A}
-modules

{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathbf{N}}\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}({}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta])^{\sigma}\oplus{}_{\mathbf{A}}K_{\eta},

where
{}_{\mathbf{A}}K_{\eta}
is the
\mathbf{A}
-submodule of
K_{\eta}
generated by
bx+\sigma(bx)

as above with
x\in{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.

Similar results hold also for the second formula in (6.9.1).

Remark 6.13.

The above results correspond to the results Lemma 3.6
\sim
Lemma 3.11 in [SZ2].
But the discussion here becomes much simpler than those used in [SZ2]
once we use the PBW-basis
\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}
.

Lemma 6.14
.

For each
\eta\in\underline{I}
, we have

(6.14.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]

\displaystyle\subset\begin{cases}\bigcap_{i\in\eta}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[i]&\quad\text{ if $\delta_{\eta}=1$, }\\
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[i]\cap\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[j]&\quad\text{ if $\delta_{\eta}=2$ with $\eta=\{i,j\}$.}\end{cases}

(6.14.2)

\displaystyle{}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]

\displaystyle\subset\begin{cases}\bigcap_{i\in\eta}{}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[i]&\quad\text{ if $\delta_{\eta}=1$, }\\
{}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[i]\cap{}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[j]&\quad\text{ if $\delta_{\eta}=2$ with $\eta=\{i,j\}$.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Assume that
\eta=\eta_{p}
with
p\leq 0
.
We consider the case where
\delta_{\eta}=2
with
\eta=\{i,j\}
,
Then
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]
is spanned by

L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
with
\mathbf{c}_{p}=\mathbb{0}
.
This basis is also expressed as a sum of the ordinary PBW-bases
L(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})
.
Since
\eta_{p}
corresponds to
(i,j,i)\in\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
, by the property of

\mathscr{X}_{\prec_{p}}
similar to (6.7.4), we see that
T_{i}^{-1}(x)\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
for

x\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]
. By the discussion using
(j,i,j)
, we also have

T_{j}^{-1}(x)\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
. Hence
x\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[i]\cap\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[j]
.
The proof for the case
\delta_{\eta}=1
is similar. Thus (i) holds. Then (ii)
follows from (i) by applying *-operation.

Next assume that
\eta=\eta_{p}
with
p>0
. In this case, by a similar
argument as above, one can prove (ii). Then (i) follows from (ii) by applying *.
The lemma is proved.
∎

Proposition 6.15
.

For each
\eta\in\underline{I}
, we have a direct sum decomposition

(6.15.1)

\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}=(\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta])^{\sigma}\oplus\widetilde{f}_{\eta}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}\oplus K_{\eta}.

The first and the second terms are orthogonal each other
with respect to the inner product
(\ ,\ )
.

Proof.

We show the first statement. This is clear if
\delta_{\eta}=1
.
We assume that
\delta_{\eta}=2
. By the proof of Proposition 2.4,

\widetilde{f}_{\eta}\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(n)}\equiv\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(n+1)}\mod K_{\eta}
, up to scalar.
Hence,
\bigoplus_{n>0}\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]\subset\widetilde{f}_{\eta}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}

modulo
K_{\eta}
. Then (6.15.1) follows from Proposition 6.12.
We show the second statement.
Assume that
\eta=\{i,j\}
with
\delta_{\eta}=2
. Then

\widetilde{f}_{\eta}=f_{i}f_{j}f_{i}
, and

\widetilde{f}_{\eta}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}\subset f_{i}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.
By Lemma 6.14,
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]\subset\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[i]
.
We know that
(\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[i],f_{i}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-})=0
, and this implies
that
(\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[i],\widetilde{f}_{\eta}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-})=0
. The lemma holds.
∎

6.16.

We define a partial order
\mathbf{c}<\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{C}_{p}
by the condition
as follows; for
\mathbf{c}=(\mathbf{c}_{+},\mathbf{c}_{0},\mathbf{c}_{-})
,

\mathbf{c}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{+},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{0},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{-})
,
\mathbf{c}<\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
if and only
if
\mathbf{c}_{+}\leq\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{+}
and
\mathbf{c}_{-}\leq\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{-}
, and at least one
of the inequalities is strict, with respect to the lexicographic
order
\leq
on
\mathbf{c}_{+}
(resp. on
\mathbf{c}_{-}
) from the left (resp. the right).
Then for each
L(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})\in\mathscr{X}_{\prec_{p}}
, there exists
a unique
b(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}
satisfying the property that

b(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
is
-
-invariant, and that

(6.16.1)

b(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})=L(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})+\sum_{\mathbf{d}>\mathbf{c}}a_{\mathbf{d}}L(\mathbf{d},\prec_{p}),

where
a_{\mathbf{d}}\in q\mathbf{Z}[q]
. Then
\pm b(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})\in\mathbf{B}
. A similar
construction also works for
L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
, and one can define the
canonical (signed) basis
b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
by the property that

b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
is
-
-invariant and that

(6.16.2)

b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})=L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})+\sum_{\mathbf{d}>\mathbf{c}}a_{\mathbf{d}}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d},\prec_{p}),

where
a_{\mathbf{d}}\in q\mathbf{Z}[q]
.

The
\mathbf{Z}[q]
-submodule
\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{Z}}(\infty)
of
\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
is defined as in
[MSZ, 1.10]. The set
\{b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})\mid\mathbf{c}\in\mathscr{C}_{p}\}

gives a
\mathbf{Z}[q]
-basis of
\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{Z}}(\infty)
, and
the modified PBW-bases also

\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}
gives a
\mathbf{Z}[q]
-basis of
\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{Z}}(\infty)
.

Note that the set of
\sigma
-orbit sums of
\mathbf{B}
gives a basis of

\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}
, and an
\mathbf{A}
-basis of
{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}
.
The set
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
is contained in

\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(n)}(\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta])^{\sigma}
or
in
\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}(\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta])^{\sigma}\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(n)}

by Proposition 6.12.
For
b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
, we define
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)
as the largest integer
a

such that
b\in\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}
, and
\varepsilon^{*}_{\eta}(b)
as the
largest integer
a
such that
b\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(a)}
.

Proposition 6.17
.

(i)

Let
b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
be such that
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)=0
. There exists a unique

b^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
such that

(6.17.1)

\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(n)}b\equiv b^{\prime}\mod\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(n+1)}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}\oplus K_{\eta}.

Then
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b^{\prime})=n
.
The correspondence
b\mapsto b^{\prime}
gives a bijection

(6.17.2)

\pi_{\eta,n}:\{b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\mid\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)=0\}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\{b^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\mid\varepsilon_{\eta}(b^{\prime})=n\}.

(ii)

Let
b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
be such that
\varepsilon^{*}_{\eta}(b)=0
. There exists a unique

b^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
such that

(6.17.3)

b\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(n)}\equiv b^{\prime}\mod\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(n+1)}\oplus K_{\eta}.

Then
\varepsilon^{*}_{\eta}(b^{\prime})=n
.
The correspondence
b\mapsto b^{\prime}
gives a bijection

(6.17.4)

\pi^{*}_{\eta,n}:\{b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\mid\varepsilon^{*}_{\eta}(b)=0\}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\{b^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\mid\varepsilon^{*}_{\eta}(b^{\prime})=n\}.

Proof.

We assume that

\eta=\eta_{p}
with
p\leq 0
, and consider
\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}
.
We also assume that
\eta=\{i,j\}
with
\delta_{\eta}=2
.
We prove (i).
Take
b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
such that
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)=0
.
Then there exists
b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
such that
b=\pm b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
.
We write

b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})=L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})+\sum_{\mathbf{d}>\mathbf{c}}a_{\mathbf{d}}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d},\prec_{p})

with
a_{\mathbf{d}}\in q\mathbf{Z}[q]
.
Since
b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
,
\mathbf{c}=(\mathbf{c}_{+},\mathbf{c}_{0},\mathbf{c}_{-})

with
\mathbf{c}_{p}=\mathbb{0}=(0,0,0)
, and
\mathbf{d}_{p}=(d_{1},d_{1},d_{1})
for some
d_{1}\geq 0
,
namely,
L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d},\prec_{p})=\widetilde{f}^{(d_{1})}_{\eta}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\prec_{p})

with
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{p}=\mathbb{0}
.
Since
\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})\in\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}
, by Proposition 6.12
it is written as a linear combination of
\widetilde{f}^{(a)}_{\eta}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})

modulo
K_{\eta}

for various
a
and
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
, where
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{p}=\mathbb{0}
.
It follows from the computation for
\mathbf{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}_{3})
(see the proof of Proposition 2.4)
that
\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(n)}\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(d_{1})}
is a linear combination of canonical
basis in
\mathbf{B}_{\eta}
, among them, the
\sigma
-invariant one is only
\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(d_{1}+n)}
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})

\displaystyle=L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})+\sum_{\mathbf{d}>\mathbf{c}}a_{\mathbf{d}}\begin{bmatrix}d_{1}+n\\
n\end{bmatrix}\widetilde{f}^{(d_{1}+n)}_{\eta}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\prec_{p})+z

\displaystyle=L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})+\sum_{\mathbf{d}>\mathbf{c}}a_{\mathbf{d}}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime},\prec_{p})+y+z,

with

y=\sum_{d_{1}>0}a_{\mathbf{d}}\biggl(\begin{bmatrix}d_{1}+n\\
n\end{bmatrix}-1\biggr)L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime},\prec_{p}),\qquad z\in{}_{\mathbf{A}}K_{\eta},

where
L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})=\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
,
and
L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime},\prec_{p})=\widetilde{f}^{(d_{1}+n)}_{\eta}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\prec_{p})

are elements in
\mathscr{X}^{\natural}_{\prec_{p}}
.
Write
y+z=\sum_{\mathbf{e}}c_{\mathbf{e}}b^{\natural}(\mathbf{e},\prec_{p})
with
c_{\mathbf{e}}\in\mathbf{A}
.
Since
d_{1}>0
, we have
\mathbf{e}_{p}>\mathbf{n}=(n,n,n)
if
\mathbf{e}_{p}
is
\sigma
-stable.
We write

c_{\mathbf{e}}
as
c_{\mathbf{e}}=c_{\mathbf{e}}^{+}+c_{\mathbf{e}}^{0}+c_{\mathbf{e}}^{-}
, where

c_{\mathbf{e}}^{+}\in q\mathbf{Z}[q],c_{\mathbf{e}}^{-}\in q^{-1}\mathbf{Z}[q^{-1}]
and
c_{\mathbf{e}}^{0}\in\mathbf{Z}
.
Then we have

(6.17.5)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}

\displaystyle b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})-\sum_{\mathbf{e}}(c_{\mathbf{e}}^{0}+c_{\mathbf{e}}^{-}+\overline{c_{\mathbf{e}}^{-}})b^{\natural}(\mathbf{e},\prec_{p})

\displaystyle=L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})+\sum_{\mathbf{d}>\mathbf{c}}a_{\mathbf{d}}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime},\prec_{p})+\sum_{\mathbf{e}}(c_{\mathbf{e}}^{+}-\overline{c_{\mathbf{e}}^{-}})b^{\natural}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime},\prec_{p}).

The left hand side of (6.17.5) is
-
-invariant, and the right hand side
belongs to
\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{Z}}(\infty)
, and is equal to

L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})\mod q\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{Z}}(\infty)
.
Hence by (6.16.2), this coincides with
b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})
.
It follows that

(6.17.6)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})

\displaystyle=b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})+\sum_{\mathbf{e}}(c_{\mathbf{e}}^{0}+c_{\mathbf{e}}^{-}+\overline{c_{\mathbf{e}}^{-}})b^{\natural}(\mathbf{e},\prec_{p})

\displaystyle\equiv b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma(\mathbf{e}_{p})=\mathbf{e}_{p}\\
\mathbf{e}_{p}>\mathbf{n}\end{subarray}}(c_{\mathbf{e}}^{0}+c_{\mathbf{e}}^{-}+\overline{c_{\mathbf{e}}^{-}})b^{\natural}(\mathbf{e},\prec_{p})\mod K_{\eta},

where
b^{\natural}(\mathbf{e},\prec_{p})
in the last sum corresponds to

L^{\natural}(\mathbf{e},\prec_{p})\in\widetilde{f}_{\eta}^{(n^{\prime})}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]
for some
n^{\prime}>n
.
Hence
b^{\natural}(\mathbf{e},\prec_{p})\in\widetilde{f}^{(n+1)}_{\eta}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
, and
the sum in (6.17.6) is contained in
\widetilde{f}^{(n+1)}_{\eta}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}
.
Note that since
\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}(b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p}))
is
\sigma
-stable,

b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})
and
b^{\natural}(\mathbf{e},\prec_{p})
in the last sum are
\sigma
-stable
(or its
\sigma
-orbit is contained in
K_{\eta}
).
Thus
b^{\prime}=b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})
satisfies the condition in (6.17.1).
It is clear that
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b^{\prime})=n
.

Note that
b^{\prime}=b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})
is determined uniquely from

L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
, which is the canonical (signed) basis corresponding
to the PBW-basis
\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c},\prec_{p})
.

Conversely, assume that
b^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
is such that
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b^{\prime})=n
.
Take
b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})
such that
b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})=\pm b
.
Then
L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})=\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\prec_{p})

with
\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}_{p}=\mathbb{0}
. Let
x

be the projection of
b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})
onto
\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta]
.
Then
x\equiv L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\prec_{p})\mod q\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{Z}}(\infty)
, and
x
is
\sigma
-stable.
Write
x=\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}x_{0}
with
x_{0}\in(\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}[\eta])^{\sigma}
.
Then
x_{0}\equiv L^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\prec_{p})\mod q\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{Z}}(\infty)
.
Consider
b=b^{\natural}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\prec_{p})
. Then

b
is determined uniquely from
x_{0}
by a similar condition as
in (6.16.2), hence
b
is
\sigma
-stable. Clearly
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)=0
.
It follows from the previous construction that
\pi_{\eta,n}(b)=b^{\prime}
.
The map
b^{\prime}\mapsto b
gives the inverse map of
\pi_{\eta,n}
.
Hence
\pi_{\eta,n}

is a bijection. This proves (i). The case where
\delta_{\eta}=1
is
proved similarly, and is simpler. Now (ii) follows from (i) by applying
the *-operation.

Next assume that
\eta=\eta_{p}
with
p>0
. In this case, by a similar
argument as above, we can prove (ii). Then (i) is obtained from this by
applying *-operation. The proposition is proved.
∎

The
\mathbf{A}
-version of Proposition 6.17 is given as follows;

Corollary 6.18
.

Let
b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
be such that
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)=0
.
Then there exists a unique
b^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
such that

(6.18.1)

\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}b\equiv b^{\prime}\mod\sum_{n^{\prime}>n}\widetilde{f}^{(n^{\prime})}_{\eta}{}_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-,\sigma}\oplus{}_{\mathbf{A}}K_{\eta}.

6.19.

For each
\eta\in\underline{I}
, by making use of the bijection (6.17.2),
we define a map
\widetilde{F}_{\eta}
as a composite of bijections

\pi_{\eta,n}^{-1}
and
\pi_{\eta,n+1}

\widetilde{F}_{\eta}:\{b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\mid\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)=n\}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\{b^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\mid\varepsilon_{\eta}(b^{\prime})=0\}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\{b^{\prime\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\mid\varepsilon_{\eta}(b^{\prime\prime})=n+1\}

In the case where
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)>0
, we define
\widetilde{E}_{\eta}
as the inverse
map of
\widetilde{F}_{\eta}
, and set
\widetilde{E}_{\eta}(b)=0
if
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)=0
.
The maps
\widetilde{F}_{\eta},\widetilde{E}_{\eta}:\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\to\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}\cup\{0\}

are called Kashiwara operators.

Proposition 6.20
.

For each
b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
, there exists a sequence
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{N}\in\underline{I}

such that
b=\pm\widetilde{F}_{\eta_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{F}_{\eta_{N}}1
.

Proof.

Take
b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
, and assume that
b\neq\pm 1
.
Then there exists
\eta\in\underline{I}
such that
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)\neq 0
.
In fact assume that
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)=0
for any
\eta
. By Lemma 6.14,
if
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)=0
, then
\varepsilon_{i}(b)=0
for any
i\in\eta
.
Thus
\varepsilon_{i}(b)=0
for any
i\in I
. By a well-known result (see [L1, Lemma 1.2.15]),
this implies that
b=\pm 1
.

Now take
\eta
such that
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)\neq 0
. Then

b^{\prime}=\widetilde{E}_{\eta}(b)\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}

satisfies the condition that
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b^{\prime})<\varepsilon_{\eta}(b)
.
Then the proposition follows by induction on the weight of
b
.
∎

We are now in a position to prove the surjectivity of
\Phi
in the affine case.
The proof is almost parallel
to the one in [SZ2, Proposition 2.10].

Proposition 6.21
.

Assume that
(X,\underline{X})
satisfies the condition in Theorem 2.6.
Then the map
\Phi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\to\mathbf{V}_{q}
is surjective,
hence Theorem 2.6 holds.

Proof.

The image of
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
gives a signed basis of
\mathbf{V}_{q}
.
Thus it is enough to show, for each
b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
, that

(6.21.1)

\pi(b)\in\operatorname{Im}\Phi.

Take
b\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
. Let
\nu=\nu(b)\in Q_{-}
be the weight of
b
.
We prove (6.21.1) by induction on
|\nu|
. If
|\nu|=0
, it certainly holds.
So assume that
|\nu|\geq 1
, and assume that (6.21.1) holds for
b^{\prime}

such that
|\nu(b^{\prime})|<|\nu|
.
By Proposition 6.20, there exists
\eta\in\underline{I}

such that
b=\widetilde{F}^{n}_{\eta}(b^{\prime})
for some
b^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
with

\varepsilon_{\eta}(b^{\prime})=0
. Thus
b^{\prime}
satisfies (6.21.1) by induction
hypothesis. We may further assume that
(6.21.1) holds for
b^{\prime\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
such that
\nu(b^{\prime\prime})=\nu
and that

\varepsilon_{\eta}(b^{\prime\prime})>n
(note that since
\dim(\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-})_{\nu}<\infty
,
if
n^{\prime}>>0
, there does not exist
b^{\prime\prime}
such that
\nu(b^{\prime\prime})=\nu
and that

\varepsilon_{\eta}(b^{\prime\prime})=n^{\prime}
).
By Corollary 6.18,
b
can be written as

b=\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}b^{\prime}+y+z
, where
z\in{}_{\mathbf{A}}K_{\eta}
, and

y
is an
\mathbf{A}
-linear combination of
b^{\prime\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
such that

\nu(b^{\prime\prime})=\nu
and that
\varepsilon_{\eta}(b^{\prime\prime})>n
.
By induction,
\pi(b^{\prime})\in\operatorname{Im}\Phi
. Since
\Phi
is a homomorphism by
Proposition 2.8, we have

\pi(\widetilde{f}^{(n)}_{\eta}b^{\prime})=\Phi(\underline{f}^{(n)}_{\eta})\pi(b^{\prime})\in\operatorname{Im}\Phi
.
Since
{}_{\mathbf{A}}K_{\eta}\subset J
, we have
\pi(z)=0
. By induction hypothesis,

\pi(y)\in\operatorname{Im}\Phi
. This implies that
\pi(b)\in\operatorname{Im}\Phi
.
Hence (6.21.1) holds for
\nu
. The proposition is proved.
∎

6.22.

We consider the isomorphism
\Phi:{}_{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}}\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{q}^{-}\,\raise 2.0pt\hbox{$\underrightarrow{\sim}$}\,\mathbf{V}_{q}

as in Theorem 2.6. Recall the
\mathbf{Z}
-module

(Q^{\sigma})^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{\eta\in\underline{I}}\mathbf{Z}\widetilde{\alpha}_{\eta}
, and we identify
this with
\underline{Q}=\bigoplus_{\eta\in\underline{I}}\mathbf{Z}\alpha_{\eta}
, as in 5.2.
We have weight space decompositions

\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-}=\bigoplus_{\nu\in Q_{-}}(\mathbf{U}_{q}^{-})_{\nu}
and
